#5194. AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI stocks staged a comeback Friday after a rout that swept from Wall Street to Asia. But a single green session hardly settles the bigger question for investors heading into the weekend: Is this an overdue correction, or the beginning of an AI bubble bursting?

Nvidia rose 2.78% to $186.64, after plunging 9.3% this Thursday. AMD also bounced to $449.04 following a 17.3% collapse a day earlier. Both stocks remain well below their October peaks, showing how little a sharp relief rally can repair.

It wasn’t just U.S. markets seeing red either. South Korea’s KOSPI lost 6% Friday, while Taiwan’s benchmark dropped more than 8% during the week—its worst week in months. TSMC hit its daily limit-down threshold during the rout. Reuters reported that Taiwanese officials asked state-owned banks to help support prices.

The US Tech 100 cash-market proxy also recovered, gaining 1.85% to 27,007.3, which means it’s down more than 5.5% this week. Its daily candles show the bounce arriving after a much larger slide from October levels, not a return to the previous trend.

The U.S. midterms accelerated fears that Democratic gains would mean tougher scrutiny of data centers. In 40 of 41 local ballot contests on November 3, voters favored limiting development, including by rejecting pro-construction measures, Ballotpedia found. Some votes were advisory, but the political message was unmistakable.

That matters for chip companies because data centers are their biggest growth story. Each project requires vast spending on processors, servers, power and cooling. Restrictions could delay orders or force construction elsewhere.

Yet the doubts predated the election. AI companies and their customers are spending aggressively to build capacity, often before returns are clear. More expensive borrowing makes that bet harder to sustain. Reuters quoted market strategist Steve Sosnick warning that heavily owned stocks were already “priced to perfection.”

A correction means a market has fallen at least 10% from a recent peak. It does not necessarily mean the underlying business is shrinking. Imagine a chipmaker whose shares trade at 60 times annual earnings because investors expect explosive growth. If investors become willing to pay only 40 times those same earnings, the stock can lose a third of its value without profits falling at all.

A bubble bursting is more serious: It happens when expectations prove unrealistic and the money supporting the boom begins to disappear.

History offers a useful warning. During the dot-com crash, the Nasdaq lost roughly 77% between March 2000 and October 2002. The internet was not a fad, but investors had vastly overestimated how quickly companies could turn it into profit.

Telecom operators spent fortunes laying fiber-optic cables for customers who had yet to arrive. Global Crossing, which built an international network, filed for bankruptcy in 2002. The technology survived; many companies and shareholders did not.

AI could face a similar mismatch between long-term promise and near-term returns—and some investors are already talking about it. For example, the RAM shortage is also a business opportunity that many companies are trying to use for their advantage. Former crypto miners are renting their hardware and cloud provider and inference startups are appearing everywhere.

Now, consider a hypothetical company borrowing billions to build a data center packed with Nvidia chips, expecting to rent that computing power at premium prices for years. If customers spend less than anticipated, borrowing becomes more expensive or construction is delayed, the debt still has to be repaid.

The operator may cancel chip orders, its contractors lose work, and utilities that planned new capacity lose anticipated customers. Lenders and investors holding its debt could also take losses. A stock-market selloff can then become a problem for the wider economy.

There is an important difference from 2000, however. Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon and Meta are profitable businesses with established customers and substantial cash flows, unlike many of the speculative dot-com startups. They can withstand setbacks better than smaller AI-cloud providers that depend on repeatedly borrowing to expand.

It is possible for AI to keep transforming industries while a bunch of individual AI companies fail—and for investors in successful companies to have overpaid.

The financial warning lights are beginning to flicker. Reuters reported Friday that the cost of insuring against a default by AI-cloud provider CoreWeave had climbed above 1,000 basis points. Such protection works somewhat like insurance on a loan: as the price rises, investors are signaling greater concern about repayment.

Yet broader bond markets were still operating relatively normally. That points to growing strain in the riskiest corners of AI financing, not proof of a system-wide crisis.

Next week will offer more concrete tests than another day of red or green candles. Results from CoreWeave, Applied Materials, and Cisco could reveal whether customers are still ordering capacity and equipment, while inflation data will influence expectations for borrowing costs. Stable orders and manageable debt would support the correction argument.

Repeated canceled projects, failed refinancing and shrinking sales would suggest the boom's financial foundations are cracking. The question is not whether AI has a future, but whether every company betting billions on that future can afford to get there.

#5193. NYSE Selects Avalanche for Tokenized Stock Settlement: Can AVAX Reclaim $9.00?

Home » Altcoins » NYSE Selects Avalanche for Tokenized Stock Settlement: Can AVAX Reclaim $9.00?

Wall Street’s tokenization push gives Avalanche an institutional boost, but AVAX technicals demand caution at important level.

In a major endorsement of enterprise blockchain infrastructure, the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) has chosen Avalanche (AVAX) technology as the core ledger for clearing and settling trades on its planned tokenized stock platform.

According to an Ava Labs statement, Avalanche will provide the core ledger technology for the clearing firm of the NYSE-affiliated digital trading platform.

Subject to regulatory approval, the platform is designed to enable 24/7 trading, clearing and onchain settlement of tokenized securities.

It will support equities issued across multiple blockchains, while Avalanche will provide the underlying settlement infrastructure, offering predictable performance and costs, controlled network access and regulatory flexibility.

The announcement comes amid intensifying competition in financial asset tokenization. The NYSE has expanded its transfer agent network to six firms, including Broadridge, Computershare and Superstate.

Nasdaq is also developing tokenized securities infrastructure, while DTCC launched its tokenization service in October, using a private Besu blockchain and the Canton Network.

Meanwhile, the SEC’s new innovation exemption is opening the door to tokenized equity trading, with OKX and ICE pursuing a separate initiative.

Following the announcement, AVAX broke out of its previous consolidation range but faced resistance at $8.80–$9.00, triggering a pullback. As of November 10, AVAX is trading at approximately $8.48, up 7.2% over 24 hours.

The rally pushed Bollinger Bands wider, signaling increased volatility. AVAX is now trading below the middle band at $8.62 and the moving-average reference at $8.56, suggesting that upward momentum has cooled.

Resistance stands at $8.60–$8.65 and $9.00–$9.28, while key support can be located around $8.00, followed by $7.70 and $7.30.

Over the next few days, AVAX’s direction will largely depend on whether buyers can reclaim $8.65.A recovery above $8.65 could put AVAX back on course to test $9.00. A break above $9.28, supported by stronger trading volume, would signal renewed bullish momentum.

Failure to regain near-term resistance could leave the token consolidating, while a break below $7.95 would expose lower support levels at $7.70 or $7.30.

The NYSE announcement strengthens Avalanche’s position in institutional tokenization, but its implications for AVAX remain less direct. The platform is expected to run on a dedicated blockchain built using Avalanche technology rather than the public Avalanche network.

The extent of any resulting demand for AVAX will depend on how the platform is implemented and whether it creates mechanisms for value to accrue to the token.

A sustained recovery could put $9.50–$10.00 within reach, with further resistance around $10.50–$11.50. However, failure to overcome overhead resistance could leave AVAX in a broader consolidation range.

The NYSE announcement is a significant development for Avalanche’s institutional prospects, but it has yet to translate into a confirmed bullish reversal for AVAX. After the initial AVAX price rally, traders are now assessing whether buying demand can sustain the move beyond the immediate resistance level.

#5191. AI agents break safety rules by remembering harmless user context

Controlled tests found that permission for routine chores could carry into simulated high-stakes tasks, with effects varying by model.

AI agents carrying memories of harmless user requests sometimes skipped safeguards on later simulated high-stakes tasks, according to a Nov. 5 preprint.

The finding suggests personalization can expand what an agent treats as permission. Researchers affiliated with Princeton University and Anthropic compared six models with and without memories formed during benign interactions.

Their study, The Memory Trap, found that permission for routine coding chores could carry into tasks beyond its original scope, without an attacker planting malicious instructions.

Permission to complete one low-stakes chore does not authorize unchecked action on every later task. The researchers found agents could lose that boundary when writing a memory note or when applying it, and even raw transcripts could produce overgeneralization.

Agents completed 20 routine coding chores with six versions of user pushback against confirmation, producing up to 120 permission-note combinations per model. Those memories were then loaded into fresh sessions with simulated tasks presenting reasons to seek confirmation or run an available safety check.

Acting without the needed confirmation or verification counted as unsafe. Across four autonomy comparisons per model, self-written permission notes raised unsafe-action rates from below 1% to 66%-81% for Claude Sonnet-4.6 and 35%-58% for Claude Opus-4.7.

OpenAI's GPT-5.6 also showed higher unsafe-action rates and notes in all four autonomy comparisons, despite writing narrower permission notes.

A harmless request can lose its limits when an agent writes memory or applies it to a different task.

Preserving the words of a request does not guarantee the agent preserves its scope.

Controlled experiments used scripted interactions, simulated high-stakes tasks and automatically loaded memory. Deployment frequency is unmeasured; the diagram does not depict a live incident or a tested mitigation.

Qualitative diagram of the paper's write-time and read-time overgeneralization mechanisms. Branches are conceptual routes, not rates, a timeline or a claim that every memory causes failure.

Controlled experiments used scripted interactions, simulated high-stakes tasks and automatically loaded memory. Deployment frequency is unmeasured; the diagram does not depict a live incident or a tested mitigation.

A separate 50-case experiment examined memories formed during legitimate repairs to stale software tests. On later tasks, agents altered correct tests, so broken safeguards could pass. One defective access check gave an unknown role administrator rights.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

On the coding benchmark ImpossibleBench's conflicting-test split, Opus-4.8's cheating rate rose from 29% without memory to 74% with notes. Fable-5 barely changed, from 18% to 19%.

The experiments used scripted interactions, constructed scenarios, simulated high-stakes actions, and automatically loaded memories.

Permission boundaries have practical consequences: an OpenAI model's unauthorized Sepolia testnet spending raised a separate permission failure in October, but it did not establish persistent memory as the cause.

In a Nov. 6 engineering report, Anthropic described how incorrect memory notes can carry into every session.

The study's authors propose recording a memory's task, occasion, and permission giver, and checking with users before a remembered preference authorizes an irreversible action. Yet, those safeguards still require behavioral evaluation.

#5190. XRP Ledger Introduces Quantum-Proof Security Upgrade to Users

As the XRP Ledger developers continue to make committed efforts to make the network fully quantum-resistant in the coming year, the team has just proposed a major upgrade that has sparked a buzz across the XRP community.

Sparking fresh excitement across the XRP community earlier today, the XRP Ledger (XRPL) Foundation has disclosed plans to introduce quantum-resistant accounts to users.

The team revealed plans to introduce a new feature to the XRP Ledger to offer users strict protection against future quantum computing threats without paying additional transaction fees.

While it is yet to be implemented, the proposed design will allow users to add a NIST-approved ML-DSA-44 signing key to their accounts and disable their existing keys.

This way, it will become harder for potential quantum attacks to compromise accounts, especially those whose public keys have already been exposed.

Apart from the extra layer of protection the upgrade promises to offer users, its cost-effective design has stirred more interest among the XRP community.

Notably, users will not have to pay higher transaction fees to access the additional security, as the proposed feature allows XRP holders to strengthen their account protection without worrying about extra transaction costs.

The proposed upgrade sends relief across the crypto ecosystem as it addresses concerns about the possibility of increasingly powerful quantum computers eventually breaking some of the cryptographic systems currently used to secure blockchains and cryptocurrencies.

While the upgrade is yet to launch, the team revealed that it has scheduled a migration study for the first half of 2027.

The study is aimed at examining how existing accounts can transition to the new security system and what the process would involve for users.

The proposed security upgrade has further sparked more optimism as it is also part of Ripple's broader aim of achieving full quantum protection by 2028.

#5187. LNbits disables hosted Bitcoin services with no restart timeline

A security-related change merged into development on November 9, while GitHub still listed a September backup hotfix as the latest stable release.

Bitcoin payments and wallet-account platform LNbits has called on all users to stop their LNbits servers after reporting a wave of attacks.

Stopping those servers can interrupt payment, checkout, and tipping services while operators await security fixes and clear restart guidance. LNbits said it had security patches to push and would provide further updates.

The project's product description lists point-of-sale tools, online checkout, donation buttons, tipping, wallet accounts, and APIs. It supports both self-hosted and managed deployments over underlying Lightning funding sources.

In a separate Nov. 8 notice, LNbits said it had disabled all instances on its hosted service as a precaution and expected services to resume within 24 hours.

For self-hosted operators, the request is to stop the LNbits instance they run for Bitcoin payments. Hosted customers instead depend on the provider’s action, which LNbits said it had already taken.

Self-hosted operators were told to stop servers; LNbits said it disabled its hosted instances. At the Nov. 9 check, the latest stable release still predated the warning.

Point-in-time public material, about 18:20 UTC Nov. 9, 2026. Hosted shutdown is attributed to @lnbits; restoration was only an estimate. PR #4310 is not established as the attack remedy, and no restart clearance was visible in the reviewed notices/timeline.

As of Nov. 9, GitHub’s latest-release API still identified v1.6.2 as LNbits’ latest stable release. Published Sept. 18, its release notes describe a PostgreSQL backup hotfix.

LNbits merged pull request #4310 into its development branch on Nov. 9. The descriptions cover stronger request protections, blocking the server from making requests to itself, and disabling new-account creation by default.

No all-clear was visible on LNbits' public timeline, and neither of its two notices announced restart clearance.

Operators face two separate questions: which released version addresses the incident, and when the project will explicitly clear servers to restart.

#5186. Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Every member of Wall Street’s “Magnificent Seven” now wants its stock kept off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Apple and Meta Platforms are among 11 companies added to the list of objecting issuers in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Thursday.

They join Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Nvidia and Tesla, completing a sweep by the group of tech giants that makes up a large share of the S&P 500’s value. The other new objectors are Home Depot, Boeing, GE Vernova, Palantir, ServiceNow, S&P Global, KLA, Super Micro Computer and Nebius Group, bringing the total number of companies saying “no” to the OKX and ICE platform to 67.

Meta’s objection carries some irony. The Facebook parent once tried to launch its own stablecoin, Libra, later renamed Diem, before abandoning the project in 2022 amid regulatory pushback.

Apple had been one of the last megacaps missing from the list after Amazon, Tesla, and SpaceX objected in the venue’s Nov. 2 notice. The tally stood at 56 then, and at nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

Just as notable is who hasn’t objected. Crypto-friendly public companies such as Coinbase, Robinhood and Strategy don’t appear on the list. Coinbase launched tokenized stocks for non-U.S. users on its Base network in August. Berkshire Hathaway, Visa, and Exxon Mobil are also absent. It’s unclear whether OKXICE notified those companies.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act failed to advance in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. It’s worth noting, though, that the objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain. An outside “tokenizer” would hold real shares one-for-one through a registered broker-dealer.

With the market’s biggest names opted out, it’s unclear which major stocks the venue will be able to offer at launch, and its notices don’t list the tokens it plans to make available.

#5184. Binance Shuts Down Transfers Involving MEXC and Its Affiliates

Just a few days after the U.S. Treasury took action to cut global crypto exchange MEXC off from the U.S. financial system, Binance has also announced strict regulatory actions against the exchange.

On Tuesday, November 10, Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by volume, announced its immediate decision to halt all transfers involving MEXC and all its affiliated companies.

Binance released an official announcement earlier today, warning users that it has stopped all trading services related to MEXC following new concerns about its legitimacy.

While the restrictions were announced to take effect immediately, Binance explained that its move follows the recent decision by the U.S. Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) to designate MEXC as a Primary Money Laundering Concern.

As such, Binance has prohibited its users from directly or indirectly sending funds to, receiving funds from, or conducting transactions through Binance involving MEXC and its related entities.

The MEXC-affiliated entities mentioned by Binance include MEXC Global Limited, MEXC Estonia OÜ, MEXC Singapore Pte Ltd, MEXC Global Foundation Ltd, GIDA Technology AG, and several other companies and affiliates operating under the MEXC name.

Although MEXC is among the top 20 crypto exchanges in the world, FinCEN issued an order against MEXC on November 6, cutting the exchange off from the U.S. financial system amid recent fraud allegations.

Notably, the U.S. Treasury accused the exchange of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to sanctions evasion and cybercrime and of defrauding people of their funds.

While Binance remains the first crypto exchange to take action in accordance with FinCEN’s rule, it appears that many other exchanges are lining up to take similar action.

#5182. NYSE brings stocks to Avalanche, but bans direct wallets

Avalanche will power clearing and settlement for a planned NYSE-affiliated tokenized securities venue, pending regulatory approval.

The Avalanche blockchain will supply the ledger that records and settles trades for a planned tokenized-securities venue affiliated with the New York Stock Exchange, Ava Labs announced Nov. 9.

The upcoming alternative trading system (ATS) is intended to offer round-the-clock trading, clearing, and settlement, subject to regulatory approvals. Avalanche will provide the core ledger technology to the platform's clearing firm.

Dennis Lee, NYSE's head of digital issuance, said in the Ava Labs release that the ledger needed speed, reliability, predictable costs, developers, and the ability to “let us control who can take part.”

Lee also said the platform would support equities on a range of blockchains, so the announced clearing-ledger choice does not require every security traded on the venue to use Avalanche as its issuance chain.

In its implementation explanation, Avalanche said institutions can tailor performance, capacity, costs and network participation to their operational requirements. Avalanche presents that flexibility as a way to meet a market operator's requirements, including control over participation.

NYSE parent Intercontinental Exchange outlined the platform on Jan. 19, with distribution through non-discriminatory access for qualified broker-dealers.

The design paired NYSE's Pillar matching engine with blockchain-based post-trade systems, along with planned dollar-sized orders, stablecoin funding, and immediate settlement.

ICE's earlier tokenized-securities plan described those access and settlement features without naming the blockchains it would use. November's announcement supplies the clearing-ledger choice for that earlier plan.

NYSE's Nov. 2 update reaffirmed distribution through participating broker-dealers and said regulatory approval remained pending.

#5181. Shiba Inu (SHIB) Scammers Fake 'Ryoshi' to Push 137 Scam Tokens and Dupe Investors

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

According to longtime SHIB community member Mazrael, since the SHIB deployer wallet went silent in October 2021, at least 137 scam tokens have falsely claimed that the SHIB deployer sent them the coins. The intent of scammers is to 'fake' Shiba Inu's pseudonymous founder, "Ryoshi," and the reason behind this is to appear legitimate: If "Ryoshi" sent it, people trust it.

Mazrael explains how this trick works: a token is just a program, and it keeps its own record of transfers. The scammer can write any address into that record as the sender. So these scammers write the SHIB deployer's address, and on Etherscan it looks like "Ryoshi" sent the token. However, the real SHIB deployer (0xB8f2…FA08) hasn't sent a single transaction since October 2021.

How scammers fake "Ryoshi" Since the SHIB deployer wallet went silent in October 2021, at least 137 scam tokens have pretended it sent them their coins. Why? To look legitimate. If "Ryoshi" sent it, people trust it. How the trick works: A token is just a program, and it keeps its own record of transfers. The scammer can write ANY address into that record as the sender. So they write the SHIB deployer's address, and on Etherscan it looks like "Ryoshi" sent you their token. But the deployer never signed anything. Click the transaction itself and look at "From". That's the wallet that really sent it, and it's never the SHIB deployer. The numbers: - 9,131+ fake transfers - 2,500+ wallets hit - 0 signed by the deployer Some of the fakes: APE, three "SHIBA INU2.0" tokens, ShibChain, SHIB DAO, METARUFFY, a token literally called "Ryoshi", Caw and Baby Caw, three fake "Shibarium" tokens (a year before the real one), "BONE2.0" and three "UNLEASHED DAO" tokens. Many got shilled by OGs and ex-admins as "Ryoshi-backed" to make them look legit. The real SHIB deployer (0xB8f2…FA08) hasn't sent a single transaction since October 2021. Always check who signed the transaction, not what the token says.

'The SHIB deployer never signed anything,' Mazrael stated, urging the crypto community to click on related token transactions and examine the "From" address, saying this is the wallet that sent it and was never the SHIB deployer.

Mazrael shared the numbers, outlining over 9,131 fake transfers, over 2,500 wallets hit, and zero signed by the SHIB deployer. He went on to name some of these fake tokens, adding that some of these tokens got promoted as "Ryoshi-backed" to make them look legitimate.

This disclosure warns crypto users to always check who signed the transaction, not what the token says.

In a separate post, Mazrael explained that nothing links the SHIB deployer address to any person.

The SHIB deployer (0xB8f2…FA08) only deployed one contract: SHIB, and it hasn't sent a single transaction since October 2021. It has no public signed message on record, either on-chain or on Etherscan.

LEASH, BONE, and TREAT each came from different deployers. Mazrael indicated that BONE's deployer is shiba-swap.eth, whereas LEASH came from '0xA221…1494,' the same key that deployed StarLink, whose own site names Woof Decentra as LEASH's developer. That key kept LEASH's rebase oracle, an admin key, for five years.

#5180. This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has an unusual idea for dealing with future quantum thieves: Make them risk their own Bitcoin every time they try to steal someone else’s.

In a November 4 post to the Bitcoin development mailing list, Fields outlined “quantum landmines” and two types of auctions designed to turn attacks on vulnerable wallets into expensive gambles. He called the ideas “thought experiments,” not a completed Bitcoin upgrade.

Bitcoin owners use a secret private key to authorize transactions. A matching public key lets the network check their signatures without learning the secret.

If the whole idea seems too hard to understand, we used AI to generate a video to help you grasp it:

Today’s computers cannot realistically calculate that private key from the public one. A sufficiently powerful quantum computer could use a mathematical technique called Shor’s algorithm to do it, potentially letting a thief spend coins as if they owned them.

Some older Bitcoin outputs have exposed public keys, and certain newer formats reveal them too. Other addresses hide the key until someone spends, so the risk is not identical for every wallet.

Researchers call the hypothetical arrival of a computer capable of these attacks “Q-Day.” No publicly demonstrated machine can steal Bitcoin this way today, and nobody knows exactly when one might exist.

Imagine someone owns 1 BTC in an old, potentially vulnerable wallet. Before Q-Day, this person creates a tiny, separate quantum-safe Bitcoin output containing a hidden proof that they controlled the original private key while quantum theft was still impossible.

Think of it as a sealed ownership receipt. Outsiders can see the small output, but not the evidence tucked inside it, or even that it is a trap.

Now imagine a future thief uses a quantum computer to recover the user’s old private key. Under Fields’s suggested rules, the thief cannot simply take her 1 BTC: They must add 1 BTC of their own from a quantum-safe wallet as collateral.

Both coins enter a restricted output worth 2 BTC. The thief must wait through a challenge period—30 days in Fields’s example—before spending it freely.

The wallet owner notices the transaction and reveals the pre-Q-Day proof. The network then allows the owner to move the full 2 BTC to the quantum-safe address they committed to earlier: their original coin, plus the thief’s collateral.

The attacker has no way to know in advance which old wallets contain these hidden receipts. Even a successful quantum key recovery could become a 1 BTC loss.

The wallet owner would also need matching collateral to initiate a normal post-Q-Day move of her own legacy coin. If they have no spare BTC, the same rule intended to hurt thieves could make moving her savings difficult.

Landmines only work when an owner prepares one before Q-Day and reveals it in time. They cannot protect coins whose keys are already lost, including some of the early Bitcoin holdings widely believed to be inaccessible.

Fields proposes a second mechanism called a staked auction. Before Q-Day, someone could lock up quantum-safe Bitcoin and secretly register a bid against a specific old wallet they expect thieves to target.

Suppose an apparently abandoned wallet holds 50 BTC. A future attacker tries to move it and posts another 50 BTC as collateral; during the auction period, someone who committed a bid in advance can challenge the attempted theft.

In Fields’s example, a successful challenger claims part of the attacker’s collateral while the original vulnerable coins are destroyed rather than awarded to the bidder. The point is to deprive thieves of a reliable payday, not return the coins to a missing owner.

His third idea, a sats auction, requires no advance stake. Instead, a vulnerable-coin spend would trigger a contest in which the highest bidder gets the coins minus their bid, while the runner-up pays their bid and gets nothing.

That resembles a notorious auction game where two bidders keep raising their offers because neither wants to be the person who loses money for nothing. A thief might avoid starting a contest that could cost more than the coins they hoped to steal.

One proposed response to quantum risk is to restrict spending from outdated wallets after a migration deadline. That might prevent theft, but could also permanently lock out legitimate owners who never moved their coins.

Fields is exploring a different trade-off: Keep old coins potentially spendable, but make attempted transfers costly and open to challenges. Bitcoin cannot tell an honest owner from a quantum thief if both can produce the same valid signature.

The trade-off is severe, considering that honest owners might need large collateral deposits, inactive holders could miss their chance to object, and auction rules might burn coins belonging to people who are still alive and simply offline.

Bitcoin would first need a quantum-safe way to hold and spend funds. Wallets would then need to support hidden ownership commitments, while Bitcoin’s consensus rules would have to enforce collateral, waiting periods, special recovery transactions, and auctions.

Developers would also need to agree on when the new rules activate, how much collateral is required, and what happens to losing bids or leftover funds. Fields believes some mechanics could be introduced through a soft fork, a type of Bitcoin rule change, but has not supplied the detailed design.

None of these protections exists in Bitcoin today. Fields has published no implementation or activation schedule, and his original post leaves the auction payouts and even the desired outcome of an auction unresolved.

#5178. Google AI spots high blood pressure using ordinary watch sensors

Google's smartwatch AI detected 65.8% of hypertension cases in a study, pointing to cuff-free screening using everyday sensor data.

Google researchers and medical collaborators have used ordinary smartwatch signals to flag possible high blood pressure without calibrating the watch against an arm cuff.

A study published in Nature on Nov. 5 suggests a way to prompt clinical checks during everyday wear, although the system missed about 33% of hypertension cases.

The AI identified 65.8% of participants with hypertension according to a clinical reference test (sensitivity), while it correctly screened 90% of those without the condition as negative (specificity).

The prospective, multicenter study followed 196 participants through seven days of smartwatch monitoring and compared results with 24-hour ambulatory blood-pressure monitoring. Participants had no known cardiovascular disease and were not taking blood-pressure medication, limiting how far the findings extend to people already receiving treatment.

The 95% confidence intervals were 54% to 76.3% for sensitivity and 83.2% to 94.7% for specificity.

The research system turns seven days of pulse and motion data into a screening score. Its detection rate leaves a substantial blind spot.

Of participants with hypertension on 24-hour ambulatory monitoring, 65.8% were detected and 34.2% were missed.

Of participants without hypertension on the reference test, 90.0% screened negative and 10.0% produced false alerts.

Screening can flag a reason for a clinical check; a negative result cannot exclude hypertension.

196 participants in a selected, risk-enriched cohort, without known cardiovascular disease or antihypertensive medication. Bars show conditional study rates, not predictive values for all smartwatch users. This is screening research, not a standalone diagnosis.

Reported sensitivity 65.8% and specificity 90.0% from the 196-person prospective study. JavaScript arithmetic: 100-65.8=34.2% missed; 100-90.0=10.0% false alerts. Bar lengths are proportional to these shares; no hypothetical patient counts. 95% confidence intervals retain reported precision.

196 participants in a selected, risk-enriched cohort, without known cardiovascular disease or antihypertensive medication. Bars show conditional study rates, not predictive values for all smartwatch users. This is screening research, not a standalone diagnosis.

The system, called AI-PPG-ACC-HTN, combines optical pulse-wave measurements with accelerometer signals.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

According to Google Research's explanation, it aggregates those signals across days and combines them with the user's height to produce a probability of hypertension.

It does not report an exact systolic and diastolic blood-pressure reading. No personal cuff reading is needed to calibrate the smartwatch system, though cuff-based measurements remained the reference the researchers used to test it.

The waveform model was pretrained using 492,347 hours of unlabeled sensor data from 2,109 consenting participants.

The study supplement describes the prospective cohort as intentionally risk-enriched. In this selected group, 80.6% of positive smartwatch screens matched hypertension on the reference test, the measure known as positive predictive value.

The research involved Google Research, Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center and Harvard Medical School.

The authors describe a possible role for smartwatch screening in prompting formal assessment and confirmatory diagnosis using established cuff-based methods, with the watch prompting a clinical check.

#5176. Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

Nvidia's board of directors has opened an independent investigation following reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, the company said on Sunday.

A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside counsel, will oversee the inquiry. "While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," it said, adding that it "has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team."

We just issued a press release from our board of directors. Read the release: https://t.co/W9yXWJzbkk

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 8, 2026

The investigation follows a Financial Times report on Saturday that a U.S. government memo, written earlier this year based on intelligence information, said the Nvidia CEO had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, citing people familiar with the memo. U.S. intelligence believes she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security, the FT reported. Nvidia declined to comment to the newspaper on the memo. Huang's email to staff, sent on Sunday, did not name the person with whom he had an affair.

Lawmakers from both parties are seeking answers. Republican leaders have asked Huang to appear privately before the House China, foreign affairs and intelligence committees, according to the FT, while Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE), who will chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee from January, pledged in a tweet that "Congress will investigate and get answers."

The FT's report built on a New York Times investigation last month, which said U.S. intelligence agencies had concluded that Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, a Chinese citizen, was a Chinese intelligence asset, and that surveillance of her had revealed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. President Donald Trump was briefed on the findings roughly a year ago and chose not to act on them, according to the Times.

U.S. officials suspected Megaspeed of helping Chinese companies get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, and the Commerce Department's investigation into the company has been handed to the Justice Department, the Times reported. Megaspeed's sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion worth of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the newspaper.

Nvidia told the Times that Huang's interactions with Ms. Huang were "not unusual or unexpected," and that customers like Megaspeed receive "ordinary business information." Megaspeed said it had retained outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation, Ms. Huang did not respond to the newspaper's requests for comment, and China's embassy in Washington dismissed the allegations as "groundless smears and slanders."

In an email to employees on Sunday, Huang apologized to his family and colleagues. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote, according to a copy published by CNBC.

"Today's news will not slow NVIDIA. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead NVIDIA as CEO without interruption," the company told CNBC.

Nvidia shares were down 5% at $177.32 in premarket trading early on Monday, after closing at $186.64 on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

#5174. Schwab adds Solana, Avalanche and Chainlink as crypto trading slows

Schwab adds three crypto assets as trading activity weakens across major platforms and Wall Street seeks retail demand.

Charles Schwab added Solana, Avalanche and Chainlink to its direct crypto trading service on Nov. 9, giving customers five assets to trade just as the industry's latest disclosures show fewer active users on some platforms and smaller trades on others.

Coinbase's monthly transacting users averaged 7.4 million in the third quarter, down from 9.3 million a year earlier.

Banks and brokerages could win existing holders' business without expanding overall participation, while easier access can redistribute trading revenue even when the overall market is subdued.

Schwab's token additions follow early access in May and a rollout to all eligible retail clients in October. Its service now offers Bitcoin, Ethereum and the three newly added tokens.

Fidelity's current trading lineup includes Bitcoin, Ethereum, Fidelity Digital Dollar, Litecoin and Solana through separate Fidelity Crypto accounts. SoFi began a phased consumer rollout on Nov. 11, 2025, bringing crypto into the same app as its other financial services.

These offerings place crypto inside financial relationships customers may already have.

Coinbase disclosed its user decline in its Oct. 29 filing, attributing its year-over-year decline in monthly transacting users primarily to fewer trading users under current market conditions.

Quarterly-average MTUs: 9.3 million in Q3 2025 and 7.4 million in Q3 2026. Includes passive activity and some non-revenue transfers; this is not a count of unique investors leaving crypto.

The MTU measure includes passive activity and some non-revenue-generating transfers, and the quarterly figure averages monthly measurements.

Coinbase also warns that MTUs can overstate unique consumers. The decline measures activity on Coinbase rather than departures from crypto as a whole.

Gemini's second-quarter results put exchange trading volume at $4 billion, down from $11 billion a year earlier, while monthly transacting users increased 11% to 580,000.

More users were present, but substantially less money changed hands.

At eToro, preliminary August figures showed the invested amount per crypto trade fell to $97 from $325 a year earlier, a 70% decline. The number of crypto trades fell a much smaller 4%, to 5.3 million.

The platform's funded accounts rose 18% to 4.36 million, including 110,000 accounts from acquisitions.

Robinhood also reported third-quarter app crypto notional volume of $21 billion, down 48% year over year, and crypto transaction revenue of $120 million, down 55%.

Outside individual platforms, Gallup's June ownership survey estimated that 9% of US adults held crypto, down from 14% in 2025. Its weighted probability-based sample covered 2,043 adults.

The disclosures show softness across several businesses and in one national ownership measure, but users can hold multiple accounts, ownership differs from trading, and the reporting periods vary.

CoinGecko's third-quarter report put trading volume on the top 10 centralized spot exchanges at $1.65 trillion, down 14.2% from $1.93 trillion in the second quarter.

CoinGecko's broader average daily trading-volume measure recovered from $58 billion in July to $95 billion in September. Outstanding positions on the top 10 perpetual-futures exchanges also increased 40.6%, ending the quarter above $100 billion.

Coinbase Institutional's Nov. 2 analysis then reported that October spot volume fell 16%, while options volume rose 9%.

CoinGecko's eight-exchange liquidity report, updated Sept. 30, found Bitcoin's sampled order-book liquidity nearly 50% above its 2025 comparison, while Solana's fell more than 28.5%.

Trading activity and available market depth are moving in different directions.

In Schwab's survey released Oct. 7, six in 10 current crypto investors expected to increase their holdings over the following 12 months.

A poll by the National Cryptocurrency Association and Harris offers another indication of potential demand. In an online survey of 2,017 US adults conducted Sept. 15-16, 42% said crypto access would make them more likely to open a bank account or use it for more of their financial needs.

Even the size of the potential audience is uncertain, since Schwab reported 21% crypto ownership in its unweighted online sample of Americans ages 21-75, compared with Gallup's 9% estimate from a weighted probability-based survey of adults 18 and older.

Crypto.com's on-chain-based estimate put worldwide ownership at 774 million in June, up 4.5% from 741 million in December 2025.

A provider can see a large commercial opportunity without expecting an immediate revival in speculative turnover. The addressable holder base and the revenue generated by each active customer are separate questions.

Existing holders moving trading to a bank or brokerage would shift market share, dormant customers returning would increase activity within an existing investor base, and first-time buyers would expand participation.

Three illustrative customer routes show why a brokerage gaining business would not necessarily mean more people own crypto.

An existing holder could move trading from an exchange to a bank or brokerage. The provider gains share without necessarily adding a crypto owner.

A dormant holder could resume trading through the added access. Activity could rise within the existing investor base.

A first-time buyer could enter crypto through a familiar financial platform. This would broaden participation.

Migration shifts share, reactivation restores activity, and first-time adoption expands participation.

Evidence of a successful expansion would come from funded crypto accounts, unique active traders, assets transferred, and incremental trading volume and revenue. Disclosing whether users are new to crypto or switching providers would distinguish adoption from redistribution.

#5171. Bitcoin trading picked up, but sellers took the initiative

Bitcoin faces a key inflation test near $75,000 as Glassnode data shows sellers dominating spot trading and ETF outflows deepening.

Bitcoin's latest weekly trading data showed sellers retaining the initiative even as turnover rose, leaving its recovery near $75,000 without a clear sign that aggressive buyers were taking control.

The Nov. 9 report from blockchain analytics firm Glassnode sets a cautious starting point ahead of today's US inflation release.

Reuters reported closing gains of about 2.4% for the S&P 500 and 2.7% for the Nasdaq Composite on Nov. 9, with cheaper oil helping relieve inflation worries. Bitcoin was trading near $75,000 on Nov. 9, according to Glassnode.

Glassnode's latest Market Pulse put spot cumulative volume delta (CVD) at negative $92 million, compared with positive $7 million seven days earlier. Its spot volume indicator rose 13.3% to $6.4 billion from $5.6 billion.

CVD tracks the balance between aggressive buying and selling within the activity Glassnode measures. Negative CVD means aggressive selling outweighed aggressive buying in that measured activity.

The report's weekly indicators describe the demand conditions Bitcoin brought into the equity rally.

Glassnode's November 9 weekly snapshot shows spot activity rising while aggressive order flow turned negative, compared with seven days earlier.

Higher trading volume can accompany seller aggression. It does not by itself confirm renewed buying pressure.

Weekly report comparison, not Monday US-session data. Spot volume is an indicator, not a stated calendar-week total. Negative CVD concerns aggressive order flow within measured activity; every trade has a buyer. Separate scales do not compare dollar magnitudes across metrics.

Two separate linear bar scales use reported indicator values: spot volume 5.6 and 6.4 USD billions, CVD +6.6 and -91.8 USD millions. CVD bars share a zero baseline. Percentage change is Glassnode's reported 13.3%, not recomputed from rounded bar values.

Weekly report comparison, not Monday US-session data. Spot volume is an indicator, not a stated calendar-week total. Negative CVD concerns aggressive order flow within measured activity; every trade has a buyer. Separate scales do not compare dollar magnitudes across metrics.

Farside Investors' completed Nov. 2-6 sessions show four aggregate outflow days and one inflow day, totaling $1,282 million in net outflows. Another $201 million left the funds on Nov. 9.

Glassnode put futures open interest at $37 billion, down 1.1% from $37.4 billion seven days earlier, and described the unwind as partial. Funding payments remained elevated while short-term holders were near break-even.

A price recovery would carry more weight if improving spot CVD showed buyers taking the initiative, while positive ETF flows persisted across completed sessions.

In its Nov. 4 analysis, Glassnode placed the short-term holder cost basis at $75,700 and the True Market Mean at $77,700. The first estimates what newer holders paid, while the second estimates the cost basis of active investors.

Below, Glassnode identified a broader $73,000-$76,000 retest band and said a sustained break below $73,000 would point to longer consolidation.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics is scheduled to release October CPI today. A favorable inflation reading could ease rate expectations and support risk appetite. As the Federal Reserve explains, expected policy rates affect broader interest rates and financial conditions.

Reuters reported Bitcoin's late-Sunday fall to $73,708 amid investor worries that a Democratic-controlled Congress would toughen crypto regulation. Those concerns offer a separate reason for caution alongside the trading data.

#5169. Strive (ASST) Adds 29 Bitcoin for $2.2 Million as Weekly Buys Slow After Crossing 43,000

Strive bought another 29 bitcoin last week for about $2.2 million at an average of $75,721 per coin between Oct. 31 and Nov. 6, a Monday 8-K filing shows, lifting total holdings to 43,153 BTC from 43,124 as of Oct. 30 and capping a month that had already pushed the Dallas treasury past 40,000 coins.

Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole posted the same figures on X: “Strive acquired 29 $BTC for $2.2M at an average cost of $75,721 per bitcoin, bringing total holdings to ₿43,153.” Strive’s corporate account republished the note minutes later. The average price is inclusive of fees and expenses, the filing says.

The buy is a sharp downshift from the pace Strive set through October. The prior week alone, from Oct. 24 through Oct. 30, Strive added 124 bitcoin at about $80,526 a coin. Between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30 the company acquired 15,124 bitcoin in total, and Friday’s third-quarter results put year-to-date bitcoin yield — Strive’s measure of the percentage change in bitcoin per share — at 63.2% through Sept. 30, with an 18.5% print for the third quarter alone.

Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 before the October sprint. That climb followed multi-thousand-coin buys earlier this fall, including a 6,182-coin purchase funded with preferred proceeds, and kept Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after Strategy.

Cash and cash equivalents fell to $487.6 million as of Nov. 6 from $490.4 million a week earlier, a $2.8 million drop that roughly covers the roughly $2.2 million bitcoin spend plus fees. Strive did not break out a separate funding line for this week’s coins in the 8-K.

The preferred stack kept moving. Shares of Strive’s 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) rose by 33,141 to 7,036,559, a tally that includes shares sold through 4:00 p.m. Eastern that settle the next business day. SATA preferred stock was unchanged at 14,030,176. Effective common shares outstanding held flat at 127,331,109 after a Class A increase of 15,382 was offset by a matching Class B decline.

Strive still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred, marked at a fair value of $50.4 million as of Nov. 6, up slightly from $50.1 million a week earlier. The company closed a registered offering of 7 million SATS shares on Oct. 20 for about $609 million in net proceeds and later lined up a $1 billion SATS at-the-market program, tools that have funded the larger buys that built the stack and that still leave room to keep adding if the ATM finds buyers.

At bitcoin near $74,650, the 43,153-coin treasury is worth roughly $3.22 billion on paper. That marks the stack about 1.4% under this week’s $75,721 average purchase price, though Strive has not disclosed a companywide cost basis in Monday’s filing the way Strategy does each week.

The Strive update lands on the same Monday that Strategy disclosed its own weekly purchase. Strategy added 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million at an average of $79,012, lifting its holdings to 850,364 BTC bought for $64.16 billion at an average cost of $75,456. The two filings keep the familiar pairing in view: Strategy still dwarfs every other public corporate holder, while Strive has used preferred issuance to climb the treasury rankings with a faster bitcoin-per-share growth claim.

ASST closed Friday at $21.45, up from Thursday’s $20.59 close and still well below its Oct. 21 local high near $30.75. The 52-week range runs from $7.02 to $34.40. At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $74,650, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows. The test for Strive from here is whether another week of single-digit coin adds is a pause after October’s sprint or the new run rate once the preferred ATM is the main fuel.

#5168. Trump Says He Knows 'Nothing' About World Liberty Financial as He Defends UAE Ties

Trump Says He Knows 'Nothing' About World Liberty Financial as He Defends UAE Ties

U.S. President Donald Trump has defended his family's stake in crypto venture World Liberty Financial, saying he knows "nothing" about the firm despite being a co-owner, in an interview after last week's midterm elections.

Asked by columnist Ross Douthat why his co-ownership of a company partly owned by investors linked to the United Arab Emirates government was different from the kind of arrangement he would call corrupt if it involved the Biden family, Trump replied: "Here's the difference [... ] I'm gonna tell you why. Because I know nothing about it," according to a transcript published by CBS News.

Pressed on why he did not simply divest, Trump said most of his money is managed by Wall Street firms through index funds, and that his sons run the family business. "It's hard to say to them, 'Don't do anything anymore for four years because I'm president,'" he said.

When Douthat asked whether foreign investors in the company believe they are buying influence, Trump said: "I don't know." He argued that conflicts of interest have dogged presidents "going back to George Washington," adding that past presidents "own something, whatever crypto was at the time."

"There's crypto, and before that there was oil," Trump said, adding that, "every president has had this problem."

"I'm a bigger name than other presidents, whether we like it or not," Trump said.

World Liberty Financial, which Eric Trump announced in August 2024, issues the WLFI governance token and the USD1 stablecoin. In January, it launched World Liberty Markets, a lending and borrowing platform built on DeFi protocol Dolomite.

Its UAE links have drawn scrutiny from Democrats. Aryam Investment, a UAE-backed entity tied to the country's national security adviser, Sheikh Tahnoon bin Zayed, agreed to buy a 49% stake in World Liberty Financial for $500 million four days before Trump's January 2025 inauguration, Decrypt reported in February, citing The Wall Street Journal. The deal, signed by Eric Trump on World Liberty's behalf, reportedly preceded the administration's reversal of restrictions on advanced AI chip sales to the UAE. A World Liberty spokesperson said at the time that neither Trump nor his Middle East envoy, Steve Witkoff, had "any involvement whatsoever" in the transaction, ABC News reported. Sen. Chris Murphy (D-CT) described the arrangement in February as "potentially criminal conduct." Trump has previously said he was not aware of the deal.

The company's UAE ties have also surfaced in its legal battle with Tron founder Justin Sun. Sun's lawyers asked a federal court to sanction World Liberty, alleging the firm delayed disclosing UAE ownership that could defeat federal jurisdiction over his lawsuit. World Liberty has called Sun's legal campaign a "meritless PR stunt."

The Trump family's financial stake in the venture is set out in World Liberty's own disclosures. DT Marks DEFI LLC, an entity affiliated with Trump and certain family members, owns about 38% of the holding company behind World Liberty Financial, holds 22.5 billion WLFI tokens, and is entitled to 75% of the net proceeds from WLFI token sales, according to the company. A separate Trump-affiliated entity has an indirect claim on interest earned on the reserves backing USD1. The disclosures state that no member of the Trump family is an officer, director or employee of World Liberty, though Trump's sons have remained involved with the company, Decrypt reported earlier this year.

#5166. Core inflation excludes oil. Bitcoin investors still cannot ignore it

Stronger post-2020 oil transmission could complicate monetary easing, but second-round effects have not yet appeared.

Oil could remain an obstacle to lower borrowing costs and greater risk appetite among Bitcoin investors, even when energy is excluded from core inflation. A Federal Reserve staff study published Nov. 9 estimates a stronger link between oil-price changes and underlying inflation in data from 2021 onward, potentially complicating monetary easing.

The findings put numbers to a channel behind the financing risks discussed in CryptoSlate's recent diesel coverage. They describe a conditional inflation threat: the authors say second-round effects from the current oil shock have not yet materialized.

Excluding energy does not remove every fuel cost embedded in other products. The U.K.'s Office for National Statistics explains that core CPI strips out direct energy components while retaining indirect energy costs in prices such as hospitality, telecommunications and transportation. Businesses can therefore pass energy costs into prices outside the excluded categories.

The Fed researchers pooled Canada, the euro area, Japan, the United Kingdom and the United States, using data from 2000 through the second quarter of 2026. The U.S. measure is core personal consumption expenditures inflation; the others use core consumer price inflation.

For the period beginning in 2021, the model links a 10-percentage-point increase in relative oil-price inflation to a roughly 0.14-percentage-point increase in core inflation. In the pre-2021 period, the estimated response was about 0.01 percentage point and statistically insignificant.

Those units matter. Relative oil-price inflation measures quarterly changes in local-currency Brent prices relative to core inflation. A 10-percentage-point increase in that measure is different from a 10% rise in oil's price level. The model allows transmission through the current quarter and four previous quarters.

Energy components are excluded, while indirect costs can remain. The staff model estimates a stronger oil-to-core link in the period beginning in 2021.

The earlier sample's estimated response was about 0.01 percentage point and statistically insignificant.

The later sample's estimated response was about 0.14 percentage point. This is a pooled model estimate, not a forecast.

Persistent core pressure could delay financing relief for Bitcoin investors; the Bitcoin link remains an inference.

Forward paths are upper bounds, not official forecasts; the authors say current-shock second-round effects have not yet appeared. Dashed connector marks the inferred Bitcoin channel, not an observed response.

The main diagram is schematic, using the ONS U.K. core-CPI explanation and the Fed/IMF conditional financing channel. Bars use a common zero-based 0–0.15-percentage-point scale; no new estimate or multiplier is calculated.

Dashed connector marks the inferred Bitcoin channel, not an observed response.

The Fed authors call their forward inflation paths an upper bound rather than a central prediction, assuming elevated persistence and pass-through continue through 2028. Their pooled estimates may also capture wider post-pandemic shocks rather than a lasting change in oil's influence. These are staff findings, not official U.S. inflation forecasts.

In a separate structural simulation, the Fed staff find that stronger oil transmission combined with more persistent inflation requires materially tighter policy even as activity weakens.

That creates a possible headwind for Bitcoin through investors' willingness to take risk. An August 2023 IMF working paper found that U.S. monetary tightening reduced a common crypto-price factor through this risk-taking channel.

The Bitcoin implication is an inference from the two studies: if oil sustains core-price pressure, it could delay financing relief and constrain risk appetite. The studies do not estimate a Bitcoin price response.

The BLS schedule lists the October CPI report for Nov. 10. Persistent core pressure accompanied by reduced easing expectations would support the financing-risk interpretation; sustained disinflation and improving easing expectations would weaken it.

#5162. Analysts project Bitcoin to move just 2.5% on today’s CPI data

The historical source estimated a CPI-window move, not a cash hedge premium or a directional forecast. Its volatility readings are not live quotes.

A pre-release Bitcoin options analysis estimated a 2.5% move for a window containing the November 10 US inflation report. Its comparison of past and expected volatility did not establish whether buying protection was cheap.

The analysis reports 3,183 BTC of long liquidations, approximately $245 million, on November 5 and another 2,450 BTC, approximately $181 million, on November 8. These are forced closures of bullish Bitcoin positions, rather than totals for the entire crypto market.

The study puts Bitcoin’s 30-day realized-volatility reading at 33.7 and Deribit’s DVOL index at 40, leaving a reported gap of 6.3 volatility points. These are the study’s readings, not live quotes. It does not document the realized measure’s annualization method, limiting how precisely it can be compared with annualized DVOL.

The observations concern the period before October’s Consumer Price Index release was scheduled for November 10 at 8:30 a.m. Eastern, or 13:30 UTC. They are not a post-release market update.

Realized volatility describes swings that have already happened. Deribit defines DVOL as a forward-looking, annualized measure covering the next 30 days. Its calculation draws on option prices at expiries on either side of that horizon and interpolates between them.

The two readings describe different stretches of time. A turbulent week can lift the backward-looking measure and bring it closer to implied volatility without demonstrating that the cash price of protection has fallen.

The Daily Digits’ November 10 CryptoQuant study compares measures that look in opposite directions.

The reported gap alone cannot establish whether a particular protective option is cheap.

No exact quote time is supplied. The diagram does not forecast future swings.

Thirty markers per side represent the reported trailing and forward 30-day horizons, not prices or exact quote endpoints. The source reports 33.7, 40.03 and a 6.33-point difference. Its realized-volatility annualization method is not documented, limiting comparability. No values are recalculated. The divider is undated because the study gives no exact quote timestamp.

No exact quote time is supplied. The diagram does not forecast future swings.

DVOL condenses option pricing into a single index reading. It does not give the cash premium for a particular protective option, so it cannot alone tell a Bitcoin holder what buying protection through CPI would cost.

The Daily Digits separately estimates a 2.5% Bitcoin move, approximately $1,884, for a window containing CPI. It reports forward implied volatility of 47.9% for that window versus 38.9% afterward. The study does not disclose the exact expiry endpoints or quote timestamp, so that estimate cannot serve as a current, reproducible protection quote.

That event-window estimate also differs from DVOL’s constant 30-day horizon. Combining the two would blur the distinction between pricing around one release and pricing a month of uncertainty.

Neither measure supplies a directional forecast. Deribit’s explanation allows for large Bitcoin moves upward as well as downward, and an implied move sets no boundary on the eventual outcome.

#5161. Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase has received final approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to charter Coinbase National Trust Company, the crypto exchange said on Monday.

The OCC approved the application last Friday, according to Coinbase. The firm said the charter will let it expand its custody business, which holds digital assets on behalf of institutional clients, under a federal regulatory framework.

"Custody is foundational to our business," the company said, describing itself as the leading crypto custodian globally. The charter will "bring clear and consistent federal uniformity" to its custody operations, it added.

"Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain," Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong tweeted on Monday, calling the trust company "another step for building that foundation."

Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain.

Coinbase National Trust Company is another step for building that foundation. https://t.co/35K2yC3xpj

— Brian Armstrong (@brian_armstrong) November 9, 2026

Coinbase has offered regulated custody since 2018, when its Coinbase Custody Trust Company won a limited purpose trust charter from the New York Department of Financial Services. The new national trust company builds on that foundation while bringing its trust activities into a consistent federal framework, Coinbase said.

The approval comes seven months after the OCC gave Coinbase conditional approval in April, when the company said it was not becoming a commercial bank and would not take retail deposits or engage in fractional reserve banking. Coinbase first applied for the charter last year.

Coinbase joins a growing list of crypto firms with national trust charters. Circle won final approval in July for a trust bank to manage the reserves behind its USDC stablecoin, and Ripple received final approval last month for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin.

Others are still awaiting final approval. Jack Dorsey's Block has applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin in September, Kraken parent Payward filed its own application in May, and a trust company linked to Trump family-backed World Liberty Financial won conditional approval in August for a charter tied to its USD1 stablecoin, Reuters reported.

The approvals have drawn opposition. Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's crypto charter approvals, including Coinbase's, are illegal, and the Independent Community Bankers of America has sued the OCC in an effort to stop crypto firms from entering banking through what it describes as a side door.

#5159. Prediction market broker River Markets registers affiliate as CFTC futures commission merchant

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

River Markets was founded in May and has routed over 250 million contracts. It's backed by "top-tier venture capital firms" following a $8.5 million seed funding round.

River Markets said its affiliate, River Finance LLC, has registered with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission as a futures commission merchant (FCM) and joined the National Futures Association (NFA), allowing it to carry customer accounts and handle client funds.

The company is building a prime brokerage platform for prediction markets that aims to let institutional traders manage capital, positions, and risk across multiple exchanges and clearinghouses through a single account, with plans to support financing arrangements and permitted over-the-counter trading.

"Our conversations with the institutions entering this market give us a unique view of what they need," Cofounder and CEO Oscar Levy said in a release. "Access to event contracts isn’t enough. They need to use capital efficiently and manage risk around the clock, across exchanges. That’s the brokerage model we’re building."

River Markets was founded in May and serves hedge funds, sports funds, proprietary trading firms, and event-risk hedging companies, according to the firm. River Markets has routed over 250 million contracts and is backed by "top-tier venture capital firms" following its $8.5 million seed funding round.

Combined trading volume across Kalshi, Polymarket US, and Polymarket's international platform exceeded $500 billion in October. Prediction markets are becoming large enough to create demand for the kinds of brokerage, clearing, financing, and risk-management services traditionally associated with derivatives markets.

In July, Polymarket-affiliated entities sought an FCM and swap-firm registration through the NFA to enable U.S. customers to trade using borrowed capital. Kalshi affiliate Kinetic Markets received NFA approval as an FCM and swap firm in March. And last month, Blockchain.com applied for both a designated contract market (DCM) license and FCM registration.

Of note, Hyperliquid Strategies last week filed to become an FCM to carry U.S. client accounts for HIP-3 perpetual futures markets, an effort to help bring permissioned Hyperliquid markets to U.S. customers within a regulated framework.

#5157. U.S. Regulators Drop $250 Travel Rule Plan, Crypto Language Too

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve have formally withdrawn a 2020 proposal that would have cut the Bank Secrecy Act threshold for cross-border funds transfers from $3,000 to $250 and spelled out that the rules cover crypto, leaving the existing $3,000 line in place.

The notice, published Monday in the Federal Register, says the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Fed’s Board of Governors “will not take further action” on a proposal they first issued on Oct. 27, 2020.

That plan would have lowered the recordkeeping and Travel Rule thresholds to $250 for transfers that begin or end outside the United States. Those rules require banks and nonbank financial institutions to collect, retain and pass along customer information on transfers of $3,000 or more.

It also would have clarified the meaning of “money” in both rules so they apply to transactions involving convertible virtual currency, along with digital assets that have legal tender status to a recipient.

Per the notcie, FinCEN and the Fed received nearly 3,000 comments on the proposal, most of them substantially similar form letters. Commenters generally opposed the lower threshold, arguing that it would burden smaller institutions and could push consumers sending remittances toward less regulated channels.

Many also noted that potentially illicit transfers below $3,000 were already being flagged to FinCEN through suspicious activity reports. Several said the threshold should rise instead, citing inflation since 1995.

Sentiment on the crypto language was mixed. Companies that provide crypto services and their trade groups opposed it, citing technical limits and the lack of an international messaging standard for crypto transfers, while some banks and money services businesses supported it.

The agencies gave two reasons for pulling the proposal. More than five years have passed, and they said more public feedback would help before adopting anything similar. The withdrawal, they wrote, gives the public “certainty” that no final rule will be issued without another chance to comment.

The second reason is the crypto industry itself. The notice says crypto markets “have continued to mature” and new legal frameworks are emerging, citing the GENIUS Act stablecoin law enacted on July 18, 2025. Any future change tied to crypto, the agencies said, should be considered in light of those developments so it is “fit for purpose.”

The notice does not repeal or amend any rule. The $3,000 threshold that has applied for decades stays where it is, and so does the existing Travel Rule.

What disappears is the specific path to $250 and the proposed wording that would have written crypto into the definition of money. The agencies did not say whether they plan a new proposal or offer a timeline.

It does not signal a retreat from enforcement. On Friday, FinCEN cut off the MEXC exchange from the U.S. financial system over alleged sanctions evasion, using a different legal tool aimed at one named platform instead of a rule covering every institution.

Stablecoins are following their own track. Under the GENIUS Act, the Fed in June proposed customer identification requirements for stablecoin issuers, and FinCEN and the Treasury’s sanctions office proposed anti-money-laundering programs for the same firms in April.

For bitcoin users, the practical effect is that nothing changes: the $3,000 trigger stays, and the crypto-specific wording that industry groups opposed is gone for now.

Whether FinCEN and the Fed return with a new threshold, a crypto definition or neither will not be clear until one is published, and the agencies have committed to taking comments first.

#5156. Any Bitcoin recovery faces a cash problem as Binance’s stablecoins fall to $32 billion

In one November 10 sample around 10:42 UTC, Binance had a greater value of Bitcoin buy orders than OKX within 0.05% below each venue’s midpoint. The $32 billion reserve figure covers ERC-20 stablecoins only.

Binance's tracked ERC-20 stablecoin reserves stand near $32 billion after a substantial contraction, giving traders a reason to test the exchange's remaining buying capacity against actual Bitcoin orders. A November 10 comparison with OKX showed Binance had a higher total value of nearby Bitcoin buy orders in one narrow slice of the market, even as its tracked stablecoin balance remained below October levels.

Community analyst Novaque Research via CryptoQuant put the balance at roughly $32 billion on Nov. 10, down from about $41 billion to $42 billion in late October. Its reserve comparison also put OKX near $9 billion, with balances trending lower there. That offers little support for a simple reading that Binance's lost tracked reserves have reappeared at that rival.

Bitcoin was trading around $74,000 on Nov. 10. Coinbase's BTC-USD ticker recorded a last trade of $74,056 at 10:41:58 UTC. The reserve observations arrive after the Oct. 29 hack at Binance and subsequent reopening of withdrawals, which CryptoSlate covered last week.

In a separate Nov. 9 post, community analyst oinonen_t via CryptoQuant reported a 25.6% decline in Binance's ERC-20 stablecoin reserves from an early-October starting point to 32 billion units. The same analysis reported a smaller 7.6% drop in Bitcoin reserves to approximately 673,000 BTC.

Those figures suggest a larger proportional retreat in the tracked stablecoin balance. However, they use an early-October comparison, while Novaque's latest post refers to late October. The exact synchronized dates and wallet coverage are unspecified, so combining them would overstate what is known about the effect of the hack.

There is also a network boundary. ERC-20 balances exclude stablecoins held on other networks. Novaque cautions that wallet restructuring and cross-chain transfers may explain part of the contraction. CryptoSlate's earlier reporting on Binance's Tether routing likewise showed why a decline in identified Ethereum balances does not establish an equivalent withdrawal of customer dollars.

CryptoQuant defines exchange reserves as coins held in exchange addresses and describes stablecoin reserves as potential buying power. Potential matters here: an inventory figure does not say how much is being offered to buy Bitcoin at a particular price.

Around 10:42 UTC on Nov. 10, a snapshot of Binance's public BTC-USDT book showed approximately 1,980,000 USDT of displayed buy orders within 5 basis points, or 0.05%, below its midpoint. The near-contemporaneous OKX book showed about 980,000 USDT within the same width below its own midpoint.

The midpoint is halfway between the highest buy price and lowest sell price. Summing each eligible buy order's price multiplied by its Bitcoin quantity gives the quoted USDT value. Both returned books extended beyond the measured band, allowing a complete comparison at that width. The amounts are in USDT, distinct from Coinbase's US-dollar trade.

Binance therefore displayed more buying interest at those nearby prices in this sample. But orders can change before execution, and one pair of observations cannot establish enduring depth, deterioration from an earlier period or a return to pre-hack conditions. It measures what was displayed then, rather than how much Bitcoin buyers actually purchased.

Customer backing answers another question. Binance says its proof of reserves concerns assets held in custody for users. That measure does not identify which balances customers have committed to Bitcoin buy orders. Customer inventory, Binance's corporate resources and posted buying orders cannot be treated as one interchangeable pool.

The order-book snapshot preceded the scheduled US inflation release. The Bureau of Labor Statistics calendar listed October CPI for Nov. 10 at 8:30 a.m. Eastern, or 13:30 UTC.

For holders assessing a rebound, stronger executed spot buying alongside buy orders that are replaced as sellers execute would challenge the concern that the smaller tracked stablecoin balance means impaired trading liquidity. Continued reserve contraction accompanied by persistently thinner depth at comparable widths would strengthen that concern.
