#5083. Coinbase’s US national trust bank charter takes effect

Coinbase plans to expand its institutional crypto custody business under the federal charter and is exploring digital asset payment products.

Coinbase’s national trust bank charter took effect on Friday, according to the US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC).

The applied for the charter in October 2025 and received preliminary approval in April 2026, according to the OCC’s filing record. The charter allows Coinbase National Trust Company to hold digital assets on behalf of clients as a fiduciary, according to the OCC’s preliminary approval letter.

Coinbase plans to offer institutional clients segregated custody accounts, with access to staking, financing and trading through other Coinbase entities, according to its charter application.

Coinbase’s application also outlined plans to explore digital asset payment products and custody services that would allow clients to interact with select smart contracts.

Other crypto companies that received OCC national trust bank charters include Ripple, Circle, BitGo, Fidelity Digital Assets and Paxos.

Crypto.com and Stripe-owned Bridge received conditional approval, while Kraken and EDX Markets are still pursuing charters.

#5081. EU taps industry to set standards for tokenized securities

Applications close Nov. 27 for an industry group that aims to draft initial standards for holding and settling tokenized securities by early 2028.

The European Central Bank, European Commission and European Securities and Markets Authority are seeking nominations for an industry-led group that will draft common standards for holding and settling tokenized securities.

Eligible applicants include EU industry associations, global institutions, private firms and fintech companies. They can apply to the European Tokenisation Standards Group (ETSG) by Nov. 27, with work set to begin in January 2027, according to a release published Monday. The three EU institutions will participate as observers.

The group will develop technical and market standards to help companies hold and settle tokenized securities across different distributed ledger technology (DLT) platforms and ensure those platforms can work with existing financial systems. The group aims to produce an initial set of standards by early 2028, according to its mandate. The standards will support Appia, the Eurosystem’s initiative to develop a blueprint for Europe’s tokenized financial markets.

The institutions warned that fragmentation could limit the benefits of tokenization and said the group may recommend ways for EU authorities to encourage firms to adopt its standards.

In September, the Eurosystem launched Pontes, a system that allows financial institutions to settle wholesale transactions in tokenized assets in central bank money.

#5080. HIVE reports $79 million quarterly net loss as bitcoin mining revenue falls and AI business grows

HIVE booked a $31.2 million impairment charge against its bitcoin mining equipment during the quarter and reported $185 million in contracted GPU cloud annualized run-rate revenue.

The company said it has approximately $40 million in live GPU cloud annualized run-rate revenue. Its contracted figure is now approximately $185 million, including capacity that has yet to enter service. HIVE previously signed a three-year agreement with Bell AI Fabric supporting Cohere in June for 2,304 Nvidia Grace Blackwell GPUs. In August, HIVE also signed a $350 million deal to supply 2,016 NVIDIA Blackwell Ultra GPUs to an unnamed enterprise customer over five years.

CEO Aydin Kilic said Monday that the company had secured $129 million in financing for the Bell and Cohere GPU purchase, and he expects those GPUs to ship before the end of 2026. HIVE's near-term goal is $200 million in GPU cloud ARR in the fourth quarter of 2026, followed by a longer-term target of $700 million in total HPC ARR by the end of 2028.

The company ended September with $139 million in cash and cash equivalents and $22.6 million in digital currencies, Kilic said. HIVE currently operates approximately 440 MW of power capacity and expects to energize another 100 MW at Yguazú in Paraguay in the fourth quarter.

#5077. Binance Hack Launderers Mint Elon Musk and Animal Meme Coins to Move Stolen Crypto

Binance Hack Launderers Mint Elon Musk and Animal Meme Coins to Move Stolen Crypto

North Korea’s hackers have found a novel use for the internet’s favorite joke tokens: laundering a slice of the record $6.4 billion stolen from Binance.

According to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic, the launderers are creating their own worthless meme coins and using them to shuffle stolen funds through decentralized exchanges in a way that breaks the direct onchain link between the thief’s wallet and the one collecting the proceeds.

The launderers mint a brand-new token, pair it with a real asset like Near or a stablecoin in a liquidity pool, pay stolen funds into the pool through one set of wallets to buy the token, then sell it back through a separate set of wallets to withdraw clean-looking assets.

Because the pool sits in the middle, no stolen funds move directly from the buying wallet to the collecting one, and the receiving wallets look like ordinary traders cashing out a meme coin.

Elliptic, in research credited to co-founder Tom Robinson, said the launderers deployed the tactic on Tron and Near, with a close variant on Solana. The clearest example came on Near, where between Nov. 5 and 7 they created at least six home-made tokens, each paired with Near in its own pool.

The coins carried the usual meme-coin trappings: cartoon logos of a bow-wearing monkey, a flag-draped duck, a chimpanzee and a fox, one even featuring an “Elon 2028 President” image of Elon Musk, who has no connection to the tokens. The names ran from the crude (FUK) to a riff on dog coins (SINU).

Each token cost about 25 Near tokens, roughly $90, to create. All six followed the same arc: launched, traded heavily within hours using stolen NEAR, then abandoned, with the leftover liquidity pulled on Nov. 8.

In total, about 117,000 Near, or roughly $430,000, passed through the six tokens before much of it flowed into stablecoins or a cross-chain bridge.

The sums are modest so far, a reminder that the bulk of the haul is still parked onchain. Elliptic earlier found more than $3 billion of the stolen crypto had yet to be laundered, and that the launderers were spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds to move funds at machine speed.

Crucially, the meme-coin trick doesn’t actually hide the money. Elliptic stressed the trail remains fully traceable onchain; the maneuver only slows investigators down, buying time in what is essentially a race to move funds before they get flagged.

It’s one cheap, disposable layer among many that North Korea’s launderers stack together. Elliptic first observed the tactic last year while tracing the $1.5 billion Bybit theft, also attributed to the TraderTraitor group behind the Binance breach.

For compliance teams, the firm warned, funds arriving from a liquidity pool shouldn’t be assumed clean, especially where freshly minted tokens and a handful of trading wallets are involved.

#5073. The $2.85B Binance hackers lost shows why the UK needs stablecoin freeze rules

The Binance hack proved stablecoin freezes work. Now the UK should make clear freeze rules a condition of listing coins.

Opinion by Lissele Pratt, co-founder, Capitalixe

Last weekend, the Federal Bureau of Investigation claimed that North Korea was behind the $6.3 billion Binance hack, which analysts are calling the largest heist on record.

While I’ve read a lot about the scale of this heist, far less has been said about what went right.

Because while it is true that the perpetrators stole $6.3 billion, they haven’t actually been able to hold on to a significant proportion of those takings. $2.85 billion of the funds has been frozen.

The reason for this comes down to what the stolen money was comprised of. Of that $2.85 billion, 94% was held in stablecoins.

While this hack was terrible news all around, it did show that centralized control, oftentimes criticized by crypto purists, might actually be one of the strongest tools we have against crypto crime.

Because, unlike other assets, stablecoins can be frozen by their issuers, giving victims a real chance to stop stolen money reaching the wrong hands.

It does, however, raise an uncomfortable question. If private companies have the power to freeze billions of dollars in assets, who decides when they should be allowed to use it?

The United Kingdom now has the opportunity to do just that.

To get an idea of just how much the freeze mattered, you just have to look at what the hackers did within the first hour of their heist.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Clearly, they understood that there was a very limited window to move the stablecoins before anyone could stop them. So that’s exactly what they did.

What was frozen from the Binance hack. Source: Elliptic

Less than an hour after the hack began, they had already sold $325 million worth of stolen USDT (Tether’s stablecoin) for Ether. The reasoning behind this was to swap an asset that could be frozen with one that could not.

Luckily, stablecoin issuers were quick to act, with Tether’s first major freeze coming just 37 minutes after the stolen funds reached the hackers’ main wallet, and Circle following three minutes later, blocking the movement of USDC.

Without Tether and Circle intervening, far more of the funds could have ended up beyond their reach.

The freeze, to its credit, worked for the most part because a handful of companies decided, in real time, to use it.

The problem is that there still isn’t an agreed standard that outlines when a freeze is justified or how quickly the response needs to be, and we’re beginning to see the consequences of not having these answers.

For instance, Circle is facing a proposed class action from Drift Protocol investors who allege it failed to freeze around $230 million in stolen USDC after the April hack. Their argument is that Circle could have stopped the money from moving, but didn’t act in time.

Meanwhile, Tether is being sued in New York by two businessmen who claim it froze millions of their assets months before a seizure warrant existed. It’s important to mention that Tether has responded to this case, calling these allegations baseless.

Working in payments and banking, it’s not uncommon for me to see traditional banks freeze money and accounts. Due to this, you may argue that if a bank can do this frequently, why shouldn’t a stablecoin issuer be able to?

Stablecoin freeze timeline. Source: CertiK

My argument isn’t that they shouldn’t. But banks don’t get to decide they want to freeze assets out of nowhere. There’s usually a legal trigger behind their decision, as well as a way to challenge it.

Stablecoin issuers now hold the same power while having to act faster and across borders. So it’s about time they’re given some structure.

There is, however, a caveat with regulating stablecoin issuers. Tether and Circle, two of the largest issuers, aren’t based in the UK, so British regulators can’t simply tell them what to do.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

What Britain can control, though, is which stablecoins UK customers are allowed to buy, and they’ve already begun doing so.

In June, the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) published its cryptoassets regime. Under it, UK trading platforms must carry out due diligence and publish a disclosure document before they list a coin, and that includes stablecoins issued overseas. It comes into force on 25 October 2027.

The Bank of England is finalizing its Code of Practice for systemic stablecoin issuers by the end of this year. It covers stablecoins used widely enough for payments to matter to UK financial stability.

Neither framework establishes the clear obligations around freezing stolen funds that I believe are necessary. But both are still taking shape, giving the UK the perfect opportunity to address this gap now, rather than trying to retrofit new requirements later.

So here’s a solution that I believe would work. Before a stablecoin can be sold on a UK platform, its issuer should have to agree to two conditions.

The first being that it is contactable and ready to act. Tether and Circle did exactly that last week, but the victim was the world’s biggest exchange. A small UK platform should get the same response.

I work with a number of smaller crypto businesses that don’t have Binance’s profile or a direct line to Tether. Under the current system, they’re the ones most likely to be left waiting.

The second is that there needs to be a clear timetable for what happens next. If an issuer freezes funds, it should notify the National Crime Agency that same day. The freeze should then expire if there hasn’t been a court order put out to keep the wallet frozen.

Neither of these are extremely controversial takes. They’d give law enforcement a chance to intervene when money is stolen and also protect people from having their assets frozen indefinitely, without legal grounds.

The Binance hack has shown us that stablecoins can give victims of crypto crime a chance to stop stolen money before it’s gone for good. That shouldn’t be a privilege reserved only for the world’s biggest exchanges.

#5070. Visa adds Monad to stablecoin settlement network options

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Payments company Visa has added the Monad blockchain network as an option to its stablecoin settlement program, allowing participating issuers and acquirers to settle through the network.

Monad joins a number of blockchains Visa already supports for the program, including Avalanche, Ethereum, Solana, Stellar, Circle's Arc, Base, Canton, Polygon and Tempo. Visa announced support for the latter five in April.

Blockchain settlement allows funds to move on weekends and holidays as well, compared with traditional card settlement, which runs on a five-business-day banking schedule, the Monad Foundation said.

"Our goal is to give our partners the flexibility to settle on the blockchain networks that best meet their needs," Dan Roesbery, Visa's vice president of global onchain partnerships and GTM, said in a statement. "Adding support for Monad expands the options available to our partners and is another step toward building a multi-chain settlement infrastructure for stablecoin-enabled payments."

Visa reported an annualized stablecoin settlement run rate of $20 billion as of September 2026, up more than 15x year over year. It also had more than 225 stablecoin-linked card programs live globally.

The feature is being built directly into Monad, so private transactions can use the same liquidity as other transactions on the network, the foundation said, adding that Monad can support this without slowing down settlement.

"The next phase will be defined by whether institutions can keep sensitive flows confidential without leaving open networks," Keone Hon, co-founder and general manager of the Monad Foundation, said in a statement.

Visa has also been exploring private settlement on Canton. In June, it announced a proof of concept with stablecoin issuer Brale to test settlement using its dollar-backed SBC token. The initiative focuses on how institutions can settle onchain while controlling who can see sensitive transaction data.

#5068. Google Takes Aim at Nvidia With New AI Chips Built for Privacy and Massive Memory

The TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max join Google’s eighth-generation Tensor Processing Unit lineup, giving businesses four configurations for training and running AI models. Google says it serves nearly 100 TPU customers, from AI labs to universities.

Unlike Nvidia’s widely used graphics processing units, TPUs are purpose-built AI accelerators. Google developed them for its own services but increasingly sells access to outside companies, including on-premises customers.

“With computing moving from human to agent scale, businesses are asking for accelerators that can reason across AI models’ massive context windows,” wrote Amin Vahdat, Google’s chief technologist and senior vice president of AI and infrastructure, in Google's announcement.

TPU 8tx targets a problem that many serious businesses have when it comes to adopting AI: protecting sensitive information while a model is using it.

Data is encrypted in storage and during transmission, but processing typically requires decrypting it in memory. Google says the configuration uses hardware-level confidential computing to isolate customer data and proprietary model parameters even during operation.

The system combines TPU 8t silicon with fifth-generation AMD EPYC processors and Google’s low-latency Boardfly network. Google claims it can cut latency by up to half versus TPU 8t, with almost no performance penalty for the added protection.

Companies could fine-tune and run models on the same system instead of transferring sensitive information between clusters. However, confidential computing cannot prevent a poorly designed AI agent from mishandling data it is allowed to access.

TPU 8i Max addresses another limitation: memory. Autonomous agents analyzing data, codebases, documents or hours of video, etc; need fast access to far more information than conventional AI tasks.

Google combines two TPU 8i chips into one package, doubling computing power, on-chip SRAM and high-bandwidth memory throughput versus TPU 8i. The design packs twice as much silicon into the same rack footprint.

“Our fourth system, TPU 8i Max, is built for a different problem: the memory wall,” Vahdat wrote.

Google is open-sourcing TPU Sync, software that moves AI working memory between chips, servers and storage. It claims the system can reduce certain data-transfer delays and response startup times by more than 90%.

Google's announcement comes as Nvidia faces mounting pressure on Wall Street. Shares plunged 9.3% on Thursday and fell another 5% in Monday's trading after CEO Jensen Huang admitted to an extramarital affair with a woman suspected by U.S. intelligence of spying for China. Nvidia's board has launched an independent investigation, although the company says it has no evidence that corporate secrets were compromised.

The scandal adds to concerns about Nvidia's growth as competitors target its AI chip dominance. OpenAI is reportedly planning to purchase Google's TPUs to reduce its reliance on Nvidia. This is still unconfirmed, but Google's expanding chip lineup could give AI developers more bargaining power, threatening Nvidia's market share and future growth expectations.

Google is competing on specialized hardware and software, not just chip speed. Its open-source TorchTPU project also lets developers run PyTorch and Hugging Face models on TPUs without rewriting their applications.

Still, Google’s performance figures compare its new systems with earlier Google hardware, not Nvidia’s latest products. The company did not disclose pricing or availability dates for the two new configurations.

#5063. Analysts see no broader ‘contagion’ from $6.3B Binance hack

Binance’s pledge to cover all user losses helped contain the fallout from the $6.3 billion hack, analysts said.

Binance’s pledge to cover user losses from its $6.3 billion hack helped contain the fallout across crypto markets, analysts told Cointelegraph.

“There doesn’t seem to be any contagion-type effect from the hack, given that Binance is able to cover the losses using its balance sheet,” Martin Lee, market insights lead at DWF Labs, told Cointelegraph. He said investors turned their focus to the implications of the US midterm elections.

Binance’s daily net outflows fell to $458 million in the 24 hours leading to Monday’s publication time, down from $6.1 billion on Nov. 2 after withdrawals resumed, according to DefiLlama.

Attackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets in the Oct. 29 breach, making it the largest crypto hack to date. Bitcoin (BTC) fell as much as 5.7% over the first five days after the hack, a decline CryptoQuant called “modest” in its Wednesday report .

Bitcoin’s drawdown after the Binance hack, compared to previous exchange hacks. Source: CryptoQuant

Major exchange hacks have previously triggered “short-lived drawdowns that markets later absorbed” as long-term holder demand returned, Lacie Zhang, research lead at Bitget Wallet, told Cointelegraph, adding that the Binance hack does not represent a “structural trend reversal” for crypto markets.

Related: Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

“Much of the hack’s impact now looks priced in,” with Bitcoin currently trading at an “attractive level,” Bitfinex analysts told Cointelegraph.

Market participants priced in an 80% chance that the Federal Reserve will raise rates at its Dec. 9 meeting, up from 69% a month earlier, according to the CMEGroup’s FedWatch tool.

#5061. AI Data Centers Are Buying Used Jet Engines as New Power Turbines Take Six Years

AI Data Centers Are Buying Used Jet Engines as New Power Turbines Take Six Years

AI data-center developers are hunting for used gas turbines and retired jet engines because ordering brand-new power equipment could leave their servers waiting for electricity until the next decade.

Some companies are buying refurbished machines, while others are converting aircraft-engine technology into generators that can run alongside their computing facilities.

New gas turbines now take an average of six years to deliver in the United States, according to energy consultancy Wood Mackenzie. Delivery discussions are already focused on 2030 and 2031, a timeline that clashes with an AI industry trying to bring enormous computing campuses online much sooner.

Global turbine orders have topped 100 gigawatts, compared with manufacturers' estimated annual capacity of just 60 to 70 gigawatts, Reuters reported. Siemens Energy, GE Vernova, and Mitsubishi Heavy Industries have all said orders are outpacing what they can produce.

Gas turbines can generate large amounts of electricity by burning natural gas to spin equipment connected to a generator. AI data centers need that power around the clock to run densely packed chips and cooling systems, and connecting new facilities to public electricity grids can itself take years.

The International Energy Agency expects global data-center electricity consumption to nearly double from 485 terawatt-hours in 2025 to 950 in 2030. Consumption at AI-focused facilities is projected to triple over the same period.

One way around the backlog is an aeroderivative turbine, a power generator built using technology adapted from aircraft jet engines.

Other companies are going further by seeking older machines and retired aircraft engines that can be rebuilt for stationary power production. For example, AI infrastructure firm Crusoe ordered 13 units from Missouri-based ProEnergy in April, each rated at 50 megawatts, for a combined 650 megawatts. The jet-engine-derived equipment is intended for future U.S. data centers, but the contract doesn’t mean that Crusoe bought engines removed from retired aircraft.

Second-hand machines can also carry a substantial discount. Used turbines in good condition sell for about $1.5 million to $1.7 million per megawatt, compared with roughly $2 million to $2.5 million for new units, according to equipment brokers and energy analysts interviewed by Reuters.

One broker described a sale of two smaller turbines totaling 29 megawatts for $12.5 million. Equivalent new equipment would have cost about $35 million and taken five years to arrive, he said.

“That secondary market is very, very hot right now,” EthosEnergy CEO Ana Amicarella told Reuters, adding that asking prices are climbing as more buyers compete for the same machinery.

Building a power plant beside a data center also brings fuel needs, air emissions, and permits into the equation. The Environmental Protection Agency says stationary turbines and engines used by data centers are subject to federal air-pollution standards.

Last week, OpenAI promised to cover electricity and grid costs at future campuses it owns and operates after voters backed tighter restrictions on data-center construction.

Power-market research firm Cleanview identified about 90 gigawatts of proposed on-site generation at U.S. data centers in its mid-2026 analysis. Only about 2 gigawatts was operating at the time

Developers are looking beyond turbines, too. Finnish manufacturer Wärtsilä told Reuters it had booked around 3 gigawatts of orders from U.S. data centers since spring 2025 for generators powered by gas engines rather than turbines.

#5058. Bitcoin, tech stocks brush off Nvidia nerves as analyst mulls $68K target

Bitcoin rebounded from an 11-week low near $73,500 as the Nasdaq Composite rallied despite sell-off in Nvidia shares.

Bitcoin (BTC) returned above $75,000 after Monday’s Wall Street open as tech stocks rebounded.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD up 0.8%, having dipped as low as $73,500 on Sunday, a level not seen since Aug. 21.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The weakness accompanied news that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang may have been the target of a Chinese intelligence effort, a claim Beijing subsequently denied. Nvidia stock fell about 6% to $170.24 on Monday early trading, a level it last traded at on March 31, before rebounding to session highs of $179.92.

Nvidia stock one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In a Sunday note, Dan Ives, senior managing director at merchant bank Yorkville Ives & Co., warned that the episode could create lasting headwinds for Nvidia’s share price.

“This is a black eye moment for Jensen and will spark multiple congressional inquiries at a minimum and will create an overhang on the stock for a myriad of reasons,” he said, according to CNBC.

Gary Gensler, former chair of the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), said that Nvidia’s board would face “tough choices” based on the results of the company’s internal investigation.

Elsewhere, investors sought to buy the lows on tech stocks, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index shaking off the Nvidia claims to gain 2.5% in the first hour of the US trading session.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Falling oil prices helped spur renewed strength in equities and offered Bitcoin some respite from its own downside. US WTI crude fell to $86.19 per barrel on the day, its lowest since Sep. 1, before recovering to around $88.20.

CFDs on WTI crude oil one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In his latest analysis, economist Robin Brooks reiterated gold, Bitcoin and the S&P 500’s negative correlation to oil following the attack on Saudi Arabia’s East-West Pipeline, a key oil-transit route, in September.

“Even if these correlations fade somewhat going forward, it’s reasonable to think that a fall in oil prices will lift these risk assets,” he summarized.

BTC/USD acted in a narrow range between its 21-week and 50-week simple moving averages (SMA) on Monday, with these at $73,078 and $75,492, respectively.

Related: BTC price eyes $73K amid Nvidia volatility: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

Commenting on current market structure, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, eyed a potential dip toward the 200-week SMA at $67,755, a trend line that last interacted with price in mid-August. At the same time, Alan saw conditions slowly starting to improve on weekly time frames.

“The macro structure and thin order books leave room for lower prices before a breakout can be validated. That test is possible, not inevitable - and it doesn’t have to happen this week. In fact, the weekly view is starting to lean bullish,” he told X followers.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21, 50, 200 SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Crypto market liquidations remained elevated during the first half of Monday, with CoinGlass reporting the 24-hour total at $1.2 billion as of 5pm UTC.

Crypto liquidation history (screenshot). Source: CoinGlass

#5055. Avalanche’s AVAX leads crypto gains following NYSE tokenization deal

Avalanche’s AVAX token rose as much as 10% after the NYSE selected its blockchain to support settlement on a planned tokenized stock trading platform.

Avalanche’s AVAX token jumped as much as 10% on Monday, making it the top earner among the 100 largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, as the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) announced plans to use its blockchain to settle trades on a new platform for trading tokenized stocks around the clock.

AVAX later eased to $8.86, still up over 8% on the day, according to CoinGecko data as of 03:22 p.m. UTC. The token currently has a market cap of around $3.95 billion, with $439.7 million traded over the past day. The Avalanche blockchain boasts a total value locked (TVL) of $633 million, up 4% over the past 24 hours, according to DeFiLlama.

“At the NYSE, we’re looking at how blockchain technology can expand what we offer in equities, and we’re picking the right technology for each job,” Dennis Lee, the head of digital issuance at NYSE, said in an announcement.

The deal underscores a growing effort by Wall Street firms to use blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and allow investors to trade outside regular market hours. Typically, traditional US stock markets are open from 9:30 am to 4:00 pm EST on weekdays, with trades often taking at least one business day to settle.

The NYSE first announced plans for the platform in January. In September, Ava Labs revealed that the exchange had spent about a year testing Avalanche, but the blockchain’s final pick was not confirmed until Monday.

Related: Aave launches V4 on Avalanche, laying groundwork for tokenized credit markets

Ava Labs said that the NYSE will use Avalanche’s blockchain to keep track of stock trades and help complete transactions on its planned trading platform. The platform will let investors buy and sell digital versions of stocks on different blockchains, while Avalanche will be used to record those trades and make sure transactions are completed.

“The upcoming digital trading platform will support equities listed on a range of blockchains, though the ledger that records and settles trades needs to be fast and reliable, let us control who can take part, keep costs predictable, and have a large community of developers behind it. Avalanche meets all those needs,” Lee said.

The platform could launch before the end of 2026 if it receives regulatory approval, according to The Wall Street Journal.

“Our first conversations with the NYSE team began nearly a year ago, and it’s remarkable how quickly we’ve progressed toward supporting this infrastructure,” John Nahas, chief business officer at Ava Labs told Cointelegraph. “It’s an important step toward blockchain becoming foundational financial infrastructure.”

The news comes as other major financial firms are also investing in blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and available around the clock.

In September, Nasdaq announced plans to invest $100 million in Kraken’s parent company, Payward, to launch tokenized stocks. The expected launch for that project is the second quarter of 2027.

Meanwhile, the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), which helps settle US securities trades, launched a service in October that allows financial firms to turn traditional securities into digital tokens.

“Today marks the culmination of years of work to bring tokenized securities into a regulated framework supported by trusted market infrastructure,” Frank La Salla, CEO of DTCC, said at the time. “By extending DTC’s resiliency, governance and operational standards into digital markets, we are establishing the foundation for responsible institutional adoption at scale.”

The NYSE deal also follows other financial firms’ efforts to bring traditional financial assets specifically onto Avalanche. In July, Japanese financial technology company Progmat announced it had moved more than 452 billion yen worth of digital securities onto a dedicated Avalanche blockchain.

And in September, Aave Labs announced plans to launch a lending service on Avalanche that would let financial firms borrow Tether’s USAT stablecoin using tokenized assets as collateral.

#5054. Billy Joel at Home: 10 Photos of the Piano Man Through the Decades

For Billy Joel, New York is more than just a place. It’s an inextricable part of what makes the singer who he is. “My speech patterns are New York. The music that I like, it was all New York–based. That’s what I think of when I think of home,” he said in his 2025 HBO documentary Billy Joel: And So It Goes. “It’s my roots.”

The Bronx native grew up in Hicksville, a middle-class suburb of Nassau County on Long Island. His family struggled to make ends meet. “There was poor, and then there was Billy Joel poor. He was lucky if he had enough money to buy a pack of cigarettes,” his childhood friend and fellow musician Jon Small said in And So It Goes. According to Joel’s sister Judy, no matter how hard times were, their mother Rosalind insisted he take piano lessons. His talent “had to be nurtured,” Judy said in the HBO doc. “She knew that he was going to be something someday.”

Before he indeed became something, Joel worked on an oyster boat. “I used to look up at this mansion on the hill and wonder what it would be like to live in a house like that,” he said in the documentary, referencing the waterfront property on Centre Island he nicknamed MiddleSea. “I used to think, ‘They’re rich bastards. They never had to work a day in their life.’ Well, I own that house now. It’s not finished yet. And neither am I.”

Read on for a glimpse into the Grammy winner’s life, from his early days in New York to his brief stint in Los Angeles and return home.

Joel’s music career took the Long Island native to Los Angeles in the early 1970s, and he signed with Columbia Records in 1973. After three years in LA, he and his first wife, Elizabeth Weber, returned to his home state, where he completed his fourth studio album, Turnstiles. “I wanted to get back to New York and make more of a rock and roll album,” Joel explained in And So It Goes. “I was on a bus on my way back to Highland Falls, New York. Elizabeth found us a house up on the Hudson River, and it was fall. It was just gorgeous. The colors were ablaze, the trees were, like, gold and red, and I remember thinking, ‘This is the New York State that I loved when I was a kid. This is magic. We don’t have this in California.’ And I wrote this love song to New York on the bus.” It would become “New York State of Mind,” one of Joel’s most popular tunes.

The singer-songwriter poses with some of his records in January 1978.

Joel’s fifth studio album, 1977’s The Stranger, was a runaway success—but the singer, pictured alongside his accolades in New York in 1978, told Playboy a few years later that his newfound fortune didn’t faze him. “I read these stories about Peter Frampton’s multimillion-dollar mansion, 15 Rolls-Royces and all that,” he said. “Who cares? It’s all bullshit. What was his last record like? Was it any good? That’s the question. People get hung up on the money thing. It’s nice to have four motorcycles. It’s fun. And I walk into the garage and go, ‘Holy shit! I’ve got four motorcycles!’ But I don’t go beyond a certain point.”

Joel, his then-wife Elizabeth, and their dog in 1978.

Instead of splurging on a million-dollar mansion, Joel told Playboy that when he and his wife decided to buy a home, “We got a $300,000 house with a mortgage like everybody else.” Their glass house, featuring floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the Long Island Sound, was located in the town of Oyster Bay on Long Island’s North Shore. Joel appears poised to hurl a rock at the home on the cover of his 1980 album, Glass Houses. “The old thing about people who live in glass houses shouldn’t throw stones—I don’t believe it,” he said. “I think, Why not? Take chances. I really wanted to throw a rock at my own house.”

Joel and Elizabeth at their Long Island residence.

In the 1970s, Joel asked Weber to be his manager. “The company I’m with now is New York–based,” Joel said of his representation. “It’s called Home Run—nothing to do with baseball. It’s a home-run agency.” Weber got right to work. She recalled in his HBO documentary that the “story [Joel] likes to tell” of her early days on the job is that he woke up the next morning to phones everywhere and “cabinets installed.” However, she said, “That’s not really true, but it’s close to true.” The couple (seen here at Joel’s grand piano in their living room) split time between their Manhattan apartment, where they worked, and Oyster Bay. Weber left her role managing Joel when the two split, and her brother Frank picked up where she left off. Home Run dissolved amid financial woes in the ’80s, and Joel eventually pressed charges against Frank over mishandled funds; the parties reached a settlement, the terms of which have not been disclosed publicly.

The musician mixing tapes in the music studio of his New York home.

The Oyster Bay house, a midcentury-modern residence, was not far from where Joel grew up in Hicksville. Photographed here in his at-home music studio in 1978, the singer’s career was on the upswing—but his relationship with Weber was growing tense. “Having a big boom in record success, I think my marriage was starting to unravel around then; 1979, it wasn’t the happiest time,” Joel once said. “Record success doesn’t translate into happiness.” Joel assumed things were paying off financially, but he admitted he “wasn’t aware” how much money he was actually bringing in. “I know we were living in a nice house, my wife was going out and buying all kinds of nice stuff,” he said. “I never really went out and did that.”

Joel multitasks in his LA home studio in 1984.

Though Joel had settled down on the East Coast, he told the Los Angeles Times that his California years cross his mind “all the time.” The singer, pictured here in LA in 1984, wrote his hit song “Piano Man” based on his experience as a lounge musician (using the name Bill Martin) at a now-defunct bar near the corner of Wilshire Boulevard and Western Avenue during the early 1970s. “I wonder what happened to the people I knew—the people in the bar in ‘Piano Man,’” he said. “It was the Executive Room in the Wilshire district. I got tips and made union scale, and I was able to afford an apartment in Studio City. The weather was great; the people were nice.”

Though it appears Joel lived like a rock star in Los Angeles—as seen here in 1984, perched on a sheepskin-style throw over a leather sofa with a pack of Marlboros and a landline telephone by his side—he told the Los Angeles Times in 2023 that he felt more like “an exiled writer living in Paris” during his early stint in the City of Angels. “I went from being an insignificant nobody to the Piano Man while I was there,” he said. “I still look back to my time in LA with a certain amount of romance.”

Joel is pictured here at the dinner table in 2001. When The New Yorker paid him a visit at his Centre Island residence in 2014, he described the interior’s aesthetic as “Catholic martyrs and saints, grotesqueries.” In the library, Joel—who decorated the place himself—had a model of the Queen Mary on his mantel, and in the living room facing the fireplace was a triptych of the Last Supper along with silver jugs and bowls on a nearby table.

Joel’s various properties have passed through some famous hands. He and his second wife, supermodel Christie Brinkley, owned a 12-acre oceanfront property in the East Hampton hamlet of Amagansett. The couple raised daughter Alexa Ray Joel in the Long Island dwelling. It was later sold to Jerry and Jessica Seinfeld for $32 million. According to The New Yorker, Joel also sold a duplex on Central Park West to singer-songwriter Sting for $5 million and a house on Martha’s Vineyard to businessman Mickey Drexler. At one point, Joel purchased a home in the Hamptons from Jaws star Roy Scheider.

“As a little kid, I always liked boats and the romance of living on an island,” Joel told Parade in 2018. The singer—pictured here on an East Hampton dock in 2001—has always felt at home on the water. In 2007 he designed a 57-foot powerboat called the Vendetta that can go about 50 knots, per The New Yorker. Boating represented “freedom,” Joel said in his documentary. “I am the captain of my ship.”

#5051. PNC Bank tests turning USDC payments into dollar deposits

The pilot lets eligible corporate and institutional clients accept USDC on Ethereum, with each token converted to one US dollar before deposit.

PNC Bank, the eighth-largest US bank by assets, launched a limited pilot enabling eligible corporate and institutional clients to receive USDC payments through Coinbase.

The pilot accepts USDC, a US dollar-backed stablecoin issued by Circle, on Ethereum using Coinbase Payments products. Payments are automatically converted into dollars and deposited into designated PNC accounts, allowing businesses to accept stablecoins without holding them.

PNC said the service aims to speed up access to funds and improve cross-border payment efficiency. The bank did not disclose fees, settlement-time benchmarks or the number of participating clients, nor did it respond to Cointelegraph’s request for additional details by publication time.

The pilot builds on a partnership established in July 2025, when PNC agreed to offer cryptocurrency services through Coinbase’s infrastructure while providing banking services to the exchange. The partnership led to the launch of Bitcoin (BTC) trading for eligible PNC Private Bank clients in December.

PNC’s move follows a similar initiative by Citi, which expanded its Coinbase partnership in September to enable institutional clients to accept stablecoin payments through its Spring by Citi platform, with Coinbase converting the payments into fiat currency.

USDC is the second-largest stablecoin by market capitalization, at $70.5 billion, behind Tether’s USDT at $176.8 billion, according to DefiLlama data. The total stablecoin market stood at around $290.8 billion on Monday.

#5050. Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.0 Are Impressive, But Be Prepared to Sell a Kidney to Afford Them

Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.0 Are Impressive, But Be Prepared to Sell a Kidney to Afford Them

Anthropic released Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 on Monday, promising AI models that can turn a handful of instructions into working software, design physical objects, and tackle research problems that previously required teams of specialists.

You can now ask a chatbot to build the app you've always wanted. You may also need to sell a kidney to pay for it.

That's not because every prompt costs thousands of dollars. It's because Fable can keep working for days, writing code, testing it, and fixing its own mistakes while the meter keeps running.

Some users have already tested its capabilities, and they’ve been impressed—by the power, and the price.

AI YouTuber Matthew Berman put the model through a series of ambitious tests and described Fable 5.2 as the “best AI model” he's ever used. His projects included playable video games and a browser-based version of Microsoft Excel, complete with formulas and pivot tables.

One single level of Gears of War: E-Day-inspired level took 43 hours and 48 minutes of build time and processed 8.63 billion tokens—small chunks of information an AI reads and produces. The running API cost estimate, based on pay-as-you-go pricing for developers, had reached $14,710.

I've burned over 100 billion tokens early testing Fable 5.2. It is BY FAR the best model I've ever used.

Here's my full review (including Gears of War clone):

> Fable 5.2 is the most autonomous model to date. Given a task with little direction, it makes… pic.twitter.com/0xTzqTY040

— Matthew Berman (@MatthewBerman) November 9, 2026

The Excel clone ran up an estimated $11,700 after discounts for cached inputs, or information the system can reuse instead of processing from scratch. Berman also showed a Fall Guys-style game that consumed more than 9 billion tokens over 71 hours, equivalent to roughly $13,000.

Those are estimated API costs, and the projects weren't flawless: Berman reported awkward animations, bugs, and features that still needed testing.

If you’re poor, well… Fable also knows how to work for cheap. For less than $40 (two months of Claude Pro) it built a cute orange cylinder.

Another experiment simulated water-balloon physics for $91. It’s worth noting this isn’t a model intended for routine tasks. This is for serious work and people with deep pockets.

In its launch announcement, Anthropic said the new models generate output up to 30% faster than their predecessors. The company also says Fable helped solve two open theoretical-physics problems involving black holes and design three functioning electronic devices, claims that will need independent scrutiny.

Anthropic's internal FrontierCode 2.0 evaluation, which measures the percentage of proposed coding changes that would be accepted into real open-source projects, put Fable 5.2 at 49.1%, compared with 32.4% for Fable 5.1. That helps explain why users are pushing it beyond simple chat responses.

Fable and Mythos share the same underlying model, but Fable has stricter safeguards around dangerous biological and cybersecurity requests. Mythos is reserved for verified organizations working in those fields.

The standard API price is $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens, down from the previous generation's $10 and $50. Optional fast mode can generate output up to 2.5 times faster, but doubles the rates to $16 and $80.

For contrast, Kimi 3.1 Pro, a model considered by many as the best Chinese AI model costs $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens. The latest Deepseek model costs $0.6 and $1.98 outside of peak hours.

Per Anthropic’s standards, Fable’s price is still twice the per-token price of Claude Opus 5.5, which Anthropic recommends for everyday work. Its flagship is meant for problems the cheaper models struggle to finish, not for asking whether your email sounds too aggressive.

Just last week, speculation swirled around a supposed Fable 5.5. The actual release is 5.2, with cheaper token rates but enough appetite to turn a hobby project into a serious expense.

Anthropic, last we checked, doesn’t accept kidneys as a method of payment.

#5049. 16 Best Early Black Friday Furniture Deals for Aesthetes (2026)

Black Friday may still be a few weeks away, but furniture retailers aren’t waiting for the official festivities to begin. Some of our favorite brands are already rolling out discounts on living room furniture, bedroom sets, and statement pieces that have absolutely no business being in our shopping cart but somehow end up there anyway. Considering how quickly foundational pieces like a new sofa, bed frame, or dresser can gobble up a decorating budget, we’re certainly not complaining.

Of course, not every early November sale is technically a Black Friday sale. Some brands are soft launching their holiday shopping season while others are tempting us with fall clearance events and limited-time promotions. Either way, there is an abundance of furniture deals worth shopping well before November 27, some of which reach up to 70% off. We’ve rounded up early Black Friday furniture deals from top retailers that should be on your home decor radar, including AD-tested favorites, designer collaborations, and plenty of pieces that might finally convince you to update that room you’ve been meaning to refresh.

A good night’s sleep is an invaluable investment, but that doesn't mean you have to pay full price for it. Saatva is currently offering $350 off orders of $1,000 or more, giving you a head start on upgrading your sleep setup. Note: You need a mattress in your cart for the discount to apply. And if a bedroom overhaul is in the cards, Saatva’s upholstered Halle storage platform bed offers a discreet place to stash extra bedding totally out of sight.

The brand’s current sale features up to 70% off select pieces, with substantial reductions on dining chairs, end tables, nightstands, and bedroom furniture. The Beacon nightstand is 40% off, and the Gilmore canopy bed, a modern minimalist’s dream, is marked down to its lowest price ever. These contemporary heirlooms are more likely to be passed down from one generation to the next than they are to go out of style, so these prices are practically a steal.

Perusing Nickey Kehoe’s sale section is akin to sifting through buried treasure. The Los Angeles design studio and retailer is known for furniture that feels thoughtful, rich with character, and difficult to pin to any one decorating era. Its Last-Chance section offers up to 30% off distinctive pieces like the architectural Iron Canopy Bed and the Community Harvest Bench. Both have the kind of lived-in appeal that makes a room look as though it’s been furnished gradually, with pieces collected over time.

If your home office has been getting by with a less-than-ideal setup, Haworth’s current sale is a good reason to treat yourself. The office furniture specialist is currently offering 25% off select performance seating, desks, gaming furniture, lighting, and accessories, with ergonomic favorites like the Fern, Zody, and Soji chairs among its most popular pieces. But, the sale extends beyond seating. The Market Wire Frame Desk offers an industrial-leaning alternative to a conventional writing table, and the Elements Laminate Flare Leg Desk brings a cleaner, more minimalist silhouette to the workspace. Considering how much time we spend at our desks, a workspace that looks as good as it works hardly seems like an unreasonable indulgence.

#5047. Mercado Bitcoin begins post-hack reopening without a timeline for withdrawals

Customers can view their pre-hack balances as access returns in phases. The exchange has yet to say when withdrawals will resume.

Crypto exchange Mercado Bitcoin began restoring read-only access to customer accounts in phases on Monday, a week after attackers drained an estimated $49 million from the platform. The exchange has yet to say when customers will be able to withdraw funds.

A Mercado Bitcoin spokesperson told Cointelegraph that access would begin at 19:00 UTC, with all accounts expected to be viewable by 21:00 UTC. Customers will be able to see their holdings as they stood on Nov. 2, the day of the hack.

In a video posted to the exchange’s X account, Fabricio Tota, vice president of new business, said Mercado Bitcoin hoped read-only access would reassure customers that their assets were still recorded on the platform.

Mercado Bitcoin, one of Brazil’s largest crypto exchanges, told Brazilian magazine Veja in late October that it had more than 4.8 million customers and had processed over 215 billion Brazilian reais ($39 billion) in cumulative transactions. The exchange says it is using its own funds to replace the stolen assets, but has yet to give a date for withdrawals to resume.

Tota explained that the company opted for “a more conservative approach” to reactivating accounts “to ensure our return happens with the utmost security.” The caution follows concerns from blockchain security analysts that the attackers were able to access addresses, draining funds through standard transfers rather than a smart contract exploit.

Blockchain analysts traced some of the stolen funds to customer deposit addresses, but Mercado Bitcoin says customers won’t bear the losses. “The vast majority of assets, in fact, nearly all of them, were never even touched by anyone; they were not even involved in the incident,” Tota said in the video.

On Nov. 4, Mercado Bitcoin pledged to cover all losses with company funds, bolstered by a $20 million investment from stablecoin issuer Tether in July. Tota highlighted that a portion of the stolen funds were also frozen in the hours after the company announced an attack had taken place.

The next phase of recovery will require users to confirm their registration details on the platform before they can move funds. The exchange plans to restore withdrawals via Pix, Brazil’s instant payment system, initially allowing customers to withdraw only to accounts in their own name. Tota gave no date for either step.

Mercado Bitcoin has yet to explain how the attackers gained access to its wallets. Blockchain data company Bitquery found that as of Nov. 4, wallets it linked to the attacker held most of the stolen funds that had escaped freezes, including 106.07 BTC.

#5046. Formation Securities plans Solana stock trading venue under SEC exemption

The proposed venue would offer 24/7 trading of tokenized shares from Galaxy Digital, Exodus and Forward Industries, with a launch planned for early 2027.

Formation Securities, a newly established company licensing technology from decentralized exchange Orca, has notified the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) of plans to launch a tokenized stock trading venue on Solana in early 2027.

The company said Monday that tokenized shares of Galaxy Digital, Exodus Movement and Forward Industries are expected to be available at launch. The venue would operate under the SEC’s temporary Innovation Exemption announced in September, which allows verified investors to trade certain US-listed shares around the clock while retaining voting, dividend and other shareholder rights.

Superstate and Securitize are expected to make tokenized shares available on the marketplace, which Formation says it intends to develop in cooperation with public companies. The venue would use automated market maker (AMM) technology, allowing investors to hold securities in their own wallets and settle trades onchain against stablecoins and other eligible assets.

Traditional transfer agents would continue to maintain official shareholder records, preserving companies’ direct relationships with shareholders.

Solana is already among the largest blockchains for tokenized equities, with approximately $575 million in tokenized stock value as of Monday, compared with $747 million on Ethereum and $1.7 billion on BNB Chain, according to RWA.xyz data.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

Formation’s issuer-focused approach comes amid growing debate over how publicly traded shares should be brought onchain. As of early November, more than 50 companies had objected to having their shares tokenized on OKXICE, a proposed venue backed by crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange.

Under the SEC’s Innovation Exemption, trading venues must notify issuers in writing and wait at least 30 calendar days before trading shares tokenized by unaffiliated third parties, giving companies an opportunity to object. Nasdaq warned in September that tokenized stock trading could have unforeseen consequences for liquidity, price discovery and long-term shareholder value.

#5045. 'Tough Questions' Ahead: Warren Targets Prediction Markets, Trump Crypto as Banking Chair

'Tough Questions' Ahead: Warren Targets Prediction Markets, Trump Crypto as Banking Chair

Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA), one of the crypto industry's most prominent critics, is preparing to use the chairmanship of the Senate Banking Committee to launch an oversight campaign aimed at Trump family crypto businesses, Big Tech and Wall Street, Politico reported on Monday.

"Big Tech CEOs, Administration officials, Trump family crypto businesses, Wall Street executives, and giant corporations should preserve their documents and prepare to answer tough questions," a Warren Banking Committee aide told the outlet.

Warren is expected to take over the committee in January after Democrats won control of the Senate in last week's midterm elections. The panel oversees banking, housing, cryptocurrency and the Federal Reserve, and its chair has broad powers to call hearings and launch investigations.

According to the aide, Warren also wants to end a "Wild West-style lack of oversight" of prediction markets that she says has allowed insiders to profit, and plans to warn about threats to financial stability, including a potential AI bubble.

She intends to keep working with Republican China hawks to restrict exports of advanced AI chips, and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is expected to be a top oversight target, Politico reported. The report came a day after Nvidia's board opened an independent investigation into reports that Huang had been the target of a Chinese intelligence effort, sending Nvidia shares sliding as much as 8%.

Warren is also likely to investigate the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency's August decision to grant a conditional bank charter to Trump-backed World Liberty Financial, as well as the administration's firings of financial agency staff and its efforts to shut down the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, Reuters reported, citing a person familiar with her thinking. "You're going to see a lot of investigations," Brian Gardner, chief Washington policy strategist at Stifel, told the outlet.

In an essay for Time last week, Warren argued that Democrats won by "being populist economic fighters," and said "Big Tech CEOs, Wall Street executives, and giant corporations should prepare to answer tough questions and face meaningful consequences if they have broken the law."

Warren has been a persistent critic of the crypto industry and of the Trump family's business interests in it. She has argued that federal regulators' approval of national trust charters for crypto firms, including Coinbase and Ripple, is illegal, and has said the CFTC has been "steamrolled" by the prediction market and crypto firms it oversees.

She has also questioned Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick over a Tether loan to a trust tied to his children, and joined Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-VT) in urging the Labor Department to drop plans that would make it easier to hold crypto in 401(k) retirement plans.

Warren was one of the leading opponents of the Clarity Act, the crypto industry's top legislative priority, which stalled in the Senate this year. Democrats are now planning to push a broader crypto market structure bill when they take control of Congress.

The White House has pushed back. A spokesperson told Reuters that Trump is willing to work with anyone on behalf of Americans, but that "sham investigations and radical left policies" would undermine any such effort.

Republicans have cast Warren as the main obstacle to crypto legislation. Sen. Tim Scott (R-SC), the current Banking chair, has argued that without market structure rules, crypto is left in "the wild, wild West."

Warren's ability to pass legislation will be limited while Trump remains in the White House. In her Time essay, she acknowledged that Trump has embraced the nickname "Mr. Veto," but argued that Democrats should send him bills anyway.

Warren is also eyeing a more senior role in Senate Democratic leadership, potentially the No. 3 position, Politico reported last month.

#5043. The World’s Most Famous Architects Are Building in Albania. I Had to Find Out Why

“I’ll try to focus on this, which is not the easiest thing today,” says Edi Rama, the embattled prime minister of Albania, after a night spent monitoring violent protests in the capital, Tirana. But Rama quickly warms to one of his favorite subjects. “Architecture,” he says, choosing his words carefully, “has the power to change societies. It’s like a strong medicine. It can heal, or it can kill.”

As a journalist who covers architecture, I had been hearing about a building boom in Albania for several years. It seemed like half the architects I knew in New York were on a first-name basis with the prime minister, a working artist and, at six-foot-seven, a former pro basketball player. So on October 19 I flew to Tirana—a short side trip from Greece, where I was on vacation with my husband—to look at some of the new buildings and interview Rama about them. But I arrived during a season of civil unrest. The prime minister, whose assistant had told me I needn’t postpone the trip because everything was fine, had moved to a secure location on the outskirts of the city; some of the buildings I had come to see had just been vandalized by protesters; and at one point I was accused, publicly, of aiding a government spy. (The charge was—need I even say this—absurd.) And, because I’m in the early stages of Parkinson’s disease, I experienced all this from a four-wheeled mobility scooter, which I had rented for about $100.

As I tooled around the city center, much of it seemed unaffected by the protests. In fact, most of Tirana was charming. But weren’t those Molotov cocktails we heard going off during an alfresco dinner? Those weren’t on my drinks menu.

What was on my menu in Tirana, a city of about 600,000 in a country of about 2.3 million, was confusion. Confusion about why the protesters were so angry, and about why the prime minister seemed so unconcerned. In his 13 years in office, Rama, 62, has been remaking as much of Albania as possible with the help of foreign architects. Even the heavily guarded villa where he agreed to be interviewed, a raw concrete building dyed bright red, is largely the work of the Spanish architect Pablo Bofill.

When protesters began demanding Rama’s resignation last May, he was about to host his second annual gathering of architects from around the world, one of the high points of his year. Architects like the Danish superstar Bjarke Ingels and the Chilean Alejandro Aravena, who won the Pritzker Prize in 2016, “pay to come here and talk, and they love it,” Rama says. But this year the get-together, from June 3 to 5, drew protesters with signs that read “Starchitects go home!” Those protests presaged the more violent demonstrations in October and November.

I haven’t attended either of Rama’s conclaves, but what I’ve heard reminds me of my visit to Ordos City, Inner Mongolia, in 2008, which I wrote about for The New York Times. A wealthy industrialist, aided by the artist Ai Weiwei, had brought 100 young architects from around the world to the isolated desert city. Their task was to design 100 villas for anonymous (and seemingly nonexistent) Chinese millionaires. Both situations felt surreal.

Rama normally works in an office on the Boulevard of Martyrs of the Nation in the center of Tirana. But on October 20 the boulevard’s sidewalks were missing hundreds of paving stones, which protesters had used to shatter storefronts. And some of the stones were reportedly thrown at police during nighttime clashes that authorities say left hundreds injured. Police have used tear gas and rubber bullets as protesters set small buildings, like an information kiosk in the vast Skanderbeg Square, on fire. Near the shell of the kiosk, workers scrubbed fresh graffiti off a statue of Skanderbeg himself. The Tirana Pyramid, built in the late 1980s by followers of the brutal dictator Enver Hoxha, and recently turned into a public amenity by the Rotterdam firm MVRDV, was also damaged.

There’s a reason some Albanians—it’s impossible to say how many—resent the foreign architects and the attention lavished on them. In the 26 years since he became the mayor of Tirana and then prime minister, Rama has initiated or encouraged more than 50 international architecture competitions for projects ranging from parks to museums to large housing developments. Forty-eight of those were won by foreign architects (some working with Albanian firms), according to Adelina Greca, who leads the National Territorial Planning Agency, which organizes competitions for the government. Only two were won by Albanian firms working alone. In addition, Rama has played matchmaker, giving private developers the names of foreign architects he strongly recommends they hire.

As a result, Tirana’s compact city center is filled with buildings in bright colors and novel shapes. There is a so-called vertical forest, a skyscraper laden with trees and shrubs, by the Italian architect Stefano Boeri. The green-colored building is just a stone’s throw from the bright red Marriott by another Italian architect, Marco Casamonti. The Hotel Plaza Tirana, by architect Johan Anrys, of the Belgian firm 51N4E, starts as an ellipse at its base and morphs into a rectangle at its crown. But the champion attention-getter is a condo building by MVRDV in the shape of a head. Its façade and balconies resolve into facial features, most conspicuously a pronounced nose. The architects have publicly identified it as the head of Skanderbeg, a 15th-century military hero. But some Albanians think it’s Rama. Both ideas are “urban legends,” says the prime minister. “It’s the form of a man through history,” he says, contradicting the architects’ own statement. Nodding at this reporter, he says, “It also looks like you.”

Tirana’s Rock (a.k.a. the Skanderbeg building) is an MVRDV-designed condo in the shape of a head

A Marriott, designed by Marco Casamonti of Archea Associati, left, and the “vertical forest” skyscraper, by Stefano Boeri

The vertical forest, the Marriott, and the Plaza Hotel are all handsome additions to the city. So, too, is the “head,” but is it too much of an architectural one-liner? Who, I wonder as I pass it several times a day, is going to want to live in the nose?

Johan Anrys, who 22 years ago became one of the first foreign architects in Albania, told me that working there has taught him “what architecture truly means for improving society.” He said he knows that Albanian architects wonder, Are we not good enough? and believe we’re taking jobs from them. “It isn’t true. I have a studio in Tirana with 20 Albanian architects.” Overall he says, the foreign architects have “brought Albanian architects more work than we’ve taken.”

Rama tells me—on-the-record, and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.” The rest, he explains, see that having first-rate architects from abroad, including Herzog & de Meuron, OMA, and Diller Scofidio & Renfro, working in Albania will raise everyone’s game.

Rama tells me—on-the-record, and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.”

The foreign architects who win competitions are required to enlist architects licensed in Albania to help them execute their plans. Rama compares the situation to that of China, which 20 years ago was giving its most prominent building commissions to foreign architects, including some of the same ones working in Albania today. Chinese architects, Rama says, got a free education from the western masters.

“If we continue this kind of cooperation,” says Greca, who has worked for Rama almost continuously since 2003, “there will come a day when Albanian architects will win competitions not only here but around the world.”

Anti-government protesters on the streets of Tirana, with the art installation Tirana Moons by Austrian architect Chris Precht in the background

But Greca’s association with Rama has made her a controversial figure among local architects. On my first day in town, our route intersects that of a lunchtime protest march. We stop to watch. Greca notices many architects and architecture students in the crowd. (The Faculty of Architecture and Urbanism (FAU) of the Polytechnic University of Tirana is nearby.)

Within minutes, Gent Progni, an organizer of the march, posts a photo of Greca and me on Instagram. The caption claims she had sunk so low as to use a disabled person (me) as cover to spy on the marchers. (Ten days later, the post has almost 7,000 “likes.”) Later, in a text responding to questions from this reporter, Progni wrote: “My opposition is not to foreign architects but to the way Edi Rama uses prestigious international names to legitimize controversial development projects, often without transparency, meaningful public consultation, or accountability. This is a significant issue for our protest movement, but it is part of a much larger struggle against corruption, the concentration of power, and the sale of Albania’s public wealth.”

Rama himself isn’t happy with all of the new buildings in Tirana. He complained about one whose façades are made of stone “until a certain altitude,” where the cost-cutting developer switched to a printed material that only looks like stone. Other buildings stand partially complete, or complete but eerily empty. And in several cases developers have replaced foreign architects with local ones midstream, in order to complete buildings without meeting the original design specifications. “We need the investors to stick with the architects,” Rama says. But funding is less available than he would like. “Certain architecture needs a certain quality,” he says, philosophically. “But compromises are forced by the limitations of the place.”

The problems with his building boom go deeper than façade materials. There are accusations of money laundering and other forms of corruption, which grew louder at the end of May, when Rama agreed to support efforts by a company associated with Jared Kushner and Ivanka Trump to build luxury resorts on previously undeveloped land. It was a coincidence that the big architecture conference started just a few days later, leading directly to the calls for starchitects to scram. Among the protesters, that demand became entangled with environmental and anti-Trump sentiments in what became known as the Flamingo Revolution.

A housing project by Madrid-based firm SelgasCano

European journalist and curator Anneke Abhelakh has recently published a brilliantly illuminating book, The Albanian Files, which gives 60 foreign architecture firms eight or more pages to describe the work they are doing in Albania. Altogether, the book lists more than 500 projects. Of those, only about one-tenth have been realized. Rama says he is looking forward to the completion of many more.

But can that really happen, given the pressure to resign? The prime minister, who won the most recent election by the biggest majority of his career, says he isn’t going anywhere.

In fact, seemingly oblivious to the mood on the street, he wants to further celebrate his stable of foreign architects. Near the end of my half-hour interview, he mentions that he is thinking of building a museum modeled after The Albanian Files, with one room for each of the 60 foreign firms to show drawings, models, and photographs of their work. He said he already has a site for what he says will be “a big store of dreams, hopes and challenges, illusions and delusions. It will be a great place to go.” Its designer will be chosen, naturally, in an international architectural competition.

I left Albania with lots of questions and thousands of photos to sort through. In one of them I have my arm around Rama. Why the show of affection? A reflexive pose when the camera comes out. But I suppose that, politics aside, I can’t hate a man who loves architecture.

Prime Minster Rama with writer Fred Bernstein

#5042. North Korean hackers create memecoins to launder funds from Binance hack: Elliptic

The blockchain firm traced stolen funds through six new tokens on NEAR, where hackers used liquidity pools to obscure transfers between wallets.

North Korean hackers created memecoins to launder cryptocurrency stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, routing roughly $430,000 through six newly launched tokens on the NEAR blockchain, according to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

Between Nov. 5 and 7, the hackers created at least six tokens on NEAR and used liquidity pools on decentralized exchanges to move stolen funds between wallets, Elliptic said. The tokens attracted almost no outside trading and were abandoned shortly after launch.

The hackers used one set of wallets to swap stolen NEAR tokens for the new memecoins, then used another set to sell those memecoins back into the pools for NEAR. The swaps let them move funds without direct wallet-to-wallet transfers, though the transactions remained traceable onchain.

How stolen NEAR moved between wallets through liquidity pools. Source: Elliptic

Elliptic also found that five of the six token creators were funded with proceeds from earlier token sales just two to 13 minutes before the next launch, linking the tokens in a chain of transactions.

“North Korea’s launderers rarely rely on a single technique,” Elliptic co-founder Tom Robinson wrote in the report. “Instead, they layer many cheap techniques, each adding a little more delay.”

Elliptic said the same laundering technique was also used following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025, when North Korean-linked actors moved at least $24 million through a newly created token on the TRON blockchain.

At the time of Elliptic’s analysis, about 11.8 million NEAR stolen in the Binance hack remained unmoved. Elliptic valued those funds at $43.7 million.
