#5040. Should you leave Australia to avoid paying 47% tax on crypto gains?

Some Australian crypto investors are moving overseas to avoid the huge CGT increase from July 2027. But there are also less drastic ways you can pay less tax.

In part one of our special on how to avoid Australia’s massive hike in capital gains taxes on crypto in July next year, we looked at borrowing against your Bitcoin or setting up a self-managed super fund (SMSF), among four ways to mitigate the impact of the taxes.While an SMSF offers Australians the greatest tax advantages, it’s only suitable for older investors, or those who are willing to lock up their crypto for decades until they retire. For younger investors, moving overseas could reduce your future crypto tax bills to 0% — but there are less drastic options to save on tax too.

Australia’s impending tax changes have already inspired crypto king Fred Schebesta, founder of Finder.com, to become a tax exile in Singapore.

From July, the top rate on real, long-term crypto gains will increase to 47%, minus the effects of inflation on the cost basis.

For upwardly mobile, single crypto investors, the “nuclear option” of leaving the country to avoid giving almost half their gains to the government will look increasingly attractive.Many of the world’s top crypto hubs — Singapore, Portugal, Malta, UAE and Hong Kong — offer 0% capital gains taxes on long-term crypto gains in an attempt to attract wealthy investors.

Also read: The one thing these 6 global crypto hubs all have in common...Should you leave Australia to avoid paying 47% tax on crypto gains?

Ben Simpson, founder of crypto advisory service Collective Shift, says some of his members have already pulled the trigger.

“Yeah, we’ve had a number of people that have sort of packed up and gone basically, ‘f—- this. I’m off to the Bahamas or whatnot,’” he says.

Andrea Yuen, CEO of Sywftx, says she is keenly aware of the tax advantages of living outside of Australia. “I lived in Singapore for 30 years. I do miss my no capital gains tax,” she says. But she didn’t move there for tax reasons, and cautions that uprooting your life to avoid a bit of tax is a pretty drastic step.

“I would never say that we should structure our lives only to think about tax outcomes,” she says. “There are many other wonderful reasons why I’ve come back to Australia and continued to live in Australia.”

Simpson has considered the idea, given he already spends half the year in America looking after Collective Shift US, but looking into the rules put him off. “You really have to cut pretty much all ties to Australia. So if you are genuinely happy to leave and don’t want to come back for the majority of the year, and cut your healthcare and cut all your ties back to Australia, then that is a strategy.”

“Bitcoin Jesus” Roger Ver gave up his US residency in 2014, and ten years later he was arrested and charged over failing to correctly report the value of his crypto holdings and paying exit taxes. He was forced to pay almost $50 million to make the case go away. Similarly, Australians leaving the country face a hefty exit tax on departure, which may require selling a decent amount of your stack.

“They tax you on the way out. If you’ve got a lot of gains or profits you’re sitting on, they’ll catch you when you leave,” says Adrian Forza, director of Crypto Tax Australia. “Arguably it’d be bigger in the future, but it’s still difficult to stomach selling off everything to leave.”

Forza explains you also have the option of paying the Australian taxes later on, but on the full value of the gain after you sell the asset. So if you expect Bitcoin to go up, you’d be best off paying the exit tax now.

Related: Australia releases draft bill to adopt OECD crypto tax reporting framework

Simpson is only 27 and the prospect of locking up his crypto portfolio for forty years holds little appeal. Instead, he trades and holds crypto via a company to benefit from the 25% company tax rate.

Running a single-person company is easier than you might think, but not worth the hassle unless you have a reasonable amount of capital and intend to make a lot of trades. It requires registering the company with ASIC and paying an annual fee, paying an accountant to produce financial statements and/or an annual company tax return and mucking about with payroll software and payday superannuation contributions if you plan to pay yourself an income. You can pay yourself a dividend at any time when you need some money.

“The company structure is good if you’re wanting to cap the capital gains tax at the company tax rate,” he says, but notes there’s a big catch: when you take funds out of the company, you need to pay the difference between the 25% company tax rate and your personal tax rate. “You still need to pay your marginal income tax rates. If you’re on the top tax rate, like 47%,” he says.

You may be wondering: if you have to pay your income tax rate anyway, which will equal the CGT rate from July, what is the benefit in paying a few thousand dollars and filling out a bunch of paperwork to run a company?

Simpson explains that the key advantage is that while you are building up your crypto portfolio by trading in and out of different coins, you only pay the 25% company rate for each sale. That means paying up to 22% less than CGT each time you exit a position, which builds into an even more significant saving over time.

“Instead of paying 47% because you’ve just taken the profits… then the CGT amount is only 25% — the company tax rate. So when you put that money back in, you’ve got more capital to play with.”

However, if you don’t trade enough, it might be taxed at 30%.

Forza says the advantages are greater for those on higher incomes.

It’s not a particularly useful structure if you just plan to just hold your Bitcoin, however. There are some potential advantages but arguably not enough of them to be worth the hassle.

The 50% CGT discount has been replaced by an indexation discount, which takes into account how much inflation affects how much you paid originally for an asset.

This means that investors who buy and hold dividend stocks or index funds for a long period will receive a bigger CGT discount. So should you drop crypto and buy a bunch of ETFs so you can pay less tax?

The short answer is no: the new CGT ‘discount’ is largely an illusion, as it simply measures your real gain after inflation.

Simpson says crypto has been hit hard by the CGT changes, but it still offers the best risk-reward opportunity.

History suggests that despite volatility and years-long bear markets, the return from investing in Bitcoin over time would still leave investors ahead of ETF investors even after paying more tax. Investing further down the risk curve with altcoins however, can either pay off handsomely or end in total disaster.

JPMorgan assumes that Bitcoin will compound by 10% on average in the years ahead. (Source JPMorgan/Bitcoin Archive)

Simpson says he convinced his younger sister, who is a basketball player on a low income, to invest in Bitcoin two years ago rather than putting money into a savings account. “She’s up like 40% or 50% in her portfolio and she’s like, ‘wow, this is going to get me so much further towards buying a house one day than sitting in cash and losing 10% [to inflation over time],” he says.

“That’s why I think Bitcoin makes so much sense to accumulate. And the tax considerations are not really there for most people I think.”

Yuen says crypto is one of the few asset classes that offers investors the chance to make double-digit returns in a few months, or to potentially double their money. Even after paying the new taxes, crypto investors with those sorts of returns will still be ahead.

“My dad always said to me, it’s a good thing, if you’ve made profits, you should be happy to pay some tax on it.”

Well maybe not happy about it, but you’ll still be making money.

Also read: How Aussie Bitcoiners can avoid 2027’s shock CGT hike, Part 1

#5039. Yoshitomo Nara Is Gen Z’s Favorite Artist—Even if They Don’t Know His Name

Four years ago, when Terrell Brown was a college student in Wheaton, Illinois, he spotted an oversized ashtray at a local Goodwill. It featured a cartoonlike illustration of a fierce-looking girl smoking a cigarette, along with the words “Too Young to Die.” It cost him $10. Brown recognized the imagery but didn’t know the name of the artist who created it. But when he posted a photo on Reddit, a reader commented, “Yoshitomo Nara is a really cool and famous artist, this is an amazing find!”

Brown, now a vintage reseller with almost 360,000 followers on TikTok, resold the ashtray, still in its original packaging, on eBay for $2,860. The episode says something about the powerful attraction—and afterlife—of Nara’s imagery. The artist’s signature figures—round-headed children with enormous eyes and expressions that oscillate between mischief, defiance, and melancholy—are deceptively simple. They often feature slogans like, “No nukes” and “Rock ’n’ roll is not dead.”

Fans include the world’s wealthiest—like Salesforce founder Marc Benioff, a longtime Yoshitomo Nara collector who supported a major retrospective of Nara’s work last year at London’s Hayward Gallery, co-organized by the Guggenheim Museum Bilbao and Museum Frieder Burda in Baden-Baden. His work has also become something of a fixture in homes featured in AD, from the Nara sculpture in Usher’s backyard to the painting hanging in Kris Jenner’s dining room. But his fans also include Gen Z’ers who love the characters without necessarily knowing much about the artist behind them. Nara’s artwork has attained a kind of cult currency, circulating through dorm rooms, online marketplaces, thrift stores, and social media, the characters becoming so familiar they seem to belong to everyone.

Stella McCartney x Yoshitomo Nara handbag

This week, as powerhouse art dealer David Zwirner unveils the first major solo exhibition of Nara’s work in New York in over a decade (Nara’s first at the gallery since leaving Pace last year), that duality is especially striking. The highly anticipated show is poised to be a museum-quality affair. Curated by Nara himself, it will feature paintings, drawings, and sculptures across the gallery’s two Chelsea locations. Yet the gallery will undoubtedly also draw a crowd of broader Nara fans—the public who adore his instantly recognizable characters, even if they know them best as decorative icons closer to the cultural territory of Hello Kitty or Miffy.

Now in his 60s and based in Tochigi Prefecture, about 100 miles north of Tokyo, Nara emerged on the international art scene in the 1990s. His creations’ simultaneously innocent and world-weary affect is central to their appeal. The characters “look cute on the surface,” says Takako Nagasawa, deputy chairwoman at Phillips auction house, “but if you read into them, they’re not cute, like the girl who has a knife hidden behind her back.” Many collectors, she notes, display Nara’s work in their children’s bedrooms. “I think that two-sidedness is the attraction, and very relevant now. We want to talk about what’s going on around the world, but we also don’t want to talk about it.”

Usher and wife Jennifer Goicoechea Raymond, in front of Yoshitomo Nara’s Peace Head in their garden

Usher’s daughter’s room, which showcases two Nara artworks

Yoshitomo Nara, Matryoshka, 2026. © Yoshitomo Nara 2026, courtesy of the artist, Equivalence Art Agency, and David Zwirner

Yoshitomo Nara painting in a home published in the July 2013 issue of AD

Nara prizes accessibility to his art and has actively participated in extending his imagery into everyday products. While other artists have created characters that appear on merchandise, like Kaws’s Companion and Jeff Koons’s balloon dog, none have endured and proliferated quite like Nara’s, which have an underlying rebelliousness that speaks to disaffected youth across generations. Yoshiki, the frontman of the rock band X Japan, is working with Nara on the packaging for his Napa Valley wines, Y by Yoshiki, adorned with the artist’s characters. Stella McCartney, a close friend of Nara’s, has previewed her third limited-edition collaboration with the artist, ranging from $100 shoelaces to a $7,900 knit scarf. The merchandise Nara designs often connects to themes in his art, like the $12 bandages, $85 snow globes, and $1,200 table lamps available at the MoMA Design Store. (He is one of the most searched artists on MoMA’s website, says Chay Costello, the museum’s associate director of merchandising and product development.)

Sweaters from Stella McCartney’s third collaboration with Yoshitomo Nara

Stella McCartney x Yoshitomo Nara collection will be available starting November 11

Content creator Nick Lowry, who bought one of Nara’s ashtrays 20 years ago on eBay for $250, posted a TikTok video a few months ago of himself removing the object from its longtime spot on his coffee table and hanging it on the wall. (The original acrylic painting on which the ashtray is based is in the collection of the Rubell Museum, in Miami.) “Objects like this are one of the only ways to own work by a living artist whose paintings now sell for more than I could ever afford,” Lowry says in the video, “so it deserved a better spot.”

Benioff describes Nara as “one of the most important artists of our time.” Like many others, Benioff came to Nara’s work through eBay. He purchased journals designed by the artist and his “little books” about the Zen Buddhist concept of Shoshin, commonly translated as “beginner’s mind.” Benioff, who met Nara through Yoshiki, now considers the artist a close friend. He owns multiple artworks by the artist, including a drawing of a grimacing girl that reads “I’m just alright,” a totemic fiberglass sculpture of two heads in a bowl, and a monumental bronze head. “It’s great that I picked them up early,” he says of the pieces. “It’s really almost impossible to get anything [on the primary market]. The best pieces all go to the museums.”

A Yoshitomo Nara painting in a home published in the January 2018 issue of AD

Yoshitomo Nara, The Little Pilgrims (Night Walking), up for auction this week at Sotheby’s

Numerous Nara works are coming to auction in conjunction with the opening of the Zwirner show. (Nara’s auction record is $25 million for his seven-and-a-half-foot canvas Knife Behind Back, sold at Sotheby’s Hong Kong in 2019.) His eight-and-a-half-foot painting Milky Lake / Thinking One, featuring a little girl with downcast eyes, is coming up for sale later this month at the online auction platform Fair Warning at an estimate of $10 million. Next week, Christie’s is offering an eight-foot-high bronze version of his character Miss Forest, for $1 million, in its 21st Century Evening Sale, and Sotheby’s is selling his painting Candy Blue Night, from 2001, at an estimate of $5 million, and a set of fiberglass sculptures, The Little Pilgrims (Night Walking), from a 1999 edition, at an estimate of $1.2 million. (The sellers, Theodore and Ruth Baum, bought the set at auction two decades ago for just $360,000.)

For the Zwirner show, Nara has paired works from his student days in Düsseldorf in the 1980s and 1990s with recent ones, demonstrating how he incorporated drawing into his practice throughout his career. He’s also exhibiting 13 oversized bronze heads, enlarged from palm-sized clay models inspired by the snow “monsters” from his childhood winters in northern rural Japan. The show coincides with the reopening last week of the Hirshhorn Sculpture Garden in Washington, DC, after a four-year renovation, where three larger aluminum versions of the heads are on view.

Nara sculptures at the reopened Hirshhorn Sculpture Garden in Washington, DC

For most artists, the distance between the audiences for Nara’s masterpieces and his mass-produced products would be difficult to bridge. But for Nara, it may be the very thing that makes him so compelling. His work can be both a million-dollar trophy and a teenager’s trinket. And while the Zwirner show draws the art world’s big collectors and curators, the next generation of fans may be discovering him somewhere else entirely: on TikTok, Instagram, or in a gift shop.

Yoshitomo Nara bandages sold at MoMA Design Store

#5037. Is Your Penis Getting Smaller as You Age? This Common Factor Could Hide One to Two Inches.

IT PROBABLY SOUNDS like a nightmare scenario to think your penis is getting shorter. Maybe not even being visible at all. But it can happen as you age, and when it does, doctors refer to it as a buried penis.

Now it's not that your penis is physically getting shorter. It's just that most of it is hidden. More on that below.

CONVINCED YOU LOST an inch? Thankfully, your penis isn’t disappearing; it’s just hidden beneath fat tissue.

Justin Dubin, MD, urologist and men’s health specialist at Baptist Health, says that generally when you can’t see the penis, it’s usually because of low abdominal tissue surrounding the penis.

Essentially, your penis is still its regular size, but it doesn’t extend outside the body. Drogo Montague, MD, a urologist in Ohio tells MH what that looks like. “There is an opening where the penis would normally hang, and the head is usually flush with skin, but there’s no shaft outside of the body.”

Now you don’t just wake up one day with inches of your penis vanished. The effect occurs on a continuum, says Dr. Montague. You might notice at first, for instance, that your penis seems to get shorter and shorter. (Though, this is not the same as the small amount of shrinkage that some men see with age, which is due to skin cells around the penis becoming less elastic.)

“This is a serious condition because it will inhibit urination, intimacy, and personal hygiene,” says Scott Miller, MD, urologist and medical director of Wellstar Urology. “It can also strongly impact self-esteem and overall emotional health.”

MOST CASES OF buried penis are due to one thing: obesity.

While the rates of buried penises haven’t been thoroughly studied, Dr. Montague sees more men in his office today dealing with the issue than he has in the past. His booked-up calendar is no coincidence. The World Health Organization reported that 1 in 7 people is living with obesity. And even though we are living in the golden age of GLP-1s, half of adults worldwide are expected to be overweight or obese by 2050.

The penis itself has two erection chambers. About one-third is inside the body and two-thirds are outside in the penile shaft. When you’re overweight or with severe obesity—and noticing a change in length—what’s changing is basically how much of your penis is inside your body and how much is outside.

“With extreme obesity, it’s like the obese body engulfs the penis. A full buried penis is when the entire penis shaft is buried below the surface of the skin,” says Dr. Montague. So it's still there, but you just can't see it all.

There are a few factors, some starting at birth.

There is a low chance of a baby being born with abnormalities in which the ligaments that attach to the penis are weak. Fortunately, Dr. Dubin says this is usually identified and surgically corrected early on.

Another cause can be “any surgery on your penis” that’s gone wrong, says Dr. Dubin. That includes circumcision, a procedure done on 71.2 percent of American guys are circumcised at birth (or even as an adult). If a doctor does not remove an appropriate amount of foreskin, there is a risk of the penis dropping back into the fatty area surrounding it.

But any procedure that involves the penis runs the risk of scar tissue and possible infection. That includes penile injections and penis enlargement.

“The ironic part is that while a lot of these things try to make your penis look bigger or be bigger, the complication is it can make your penis actually smaller and cause buried penis,” says Dr. Dubin.

Lymphedema can cause swelling in the scrotum. As lymph fluid accumulates, you might see the makings of a buried penis.

Also known as acne inversa, the skin condition can create small and extremely painful lumps under the skin. Lumps usually appear in the armpits, inner thighs, and—you guessed it—your groin. This happens because the body mistakenly attacks hair follicles in the sweat glands, causing pockets of pus and other scarring.

Sometimes when the prostate is removed due to prostate cancer surgery, men may lose about an inch of penile length. “That’s the result of pulling the urethra up to attach to the bladder,” says Dr. Montague. Still, it seems like most penile shortening rebounds as time passes after your surgery, so don't let that stop you from getting your prostate checked.

The condition occurs when scar tissue forms in the penis and it causes a curved erection. The curvature may give the appearance of a shorter length. The issue often arises because of injury to the penis during sex or micro-tears that accumulated from sports or physical accidents affecting the genital area. Fortunately, if diagnosed early, there are drugs and procedures that could straighten out the penis. Read more about it here.

WHEN YOU HAVE a buried penis, certain activities become more difficult. Right away, you might find it hard to pee. Dr. Dubin says guys may not be able to get the stream started and might have poor flow. Buried penis can also cause trouble in the form of dribbling and incontinence.

The area is also tough to clean, which leads to a host of issues that might trigger fungal or bacterial infections or skin breakdown. Buried penis is associated, for example, with a rare skin condition called lichen sclerosus. It’s an inflammatory disorder that causes itchy patches. The skin that hosts these patches becomes very delicate.

Beyond that, penetrative sex remains an ongoing issue. “These men can’t have sex or sex is very difficult,” says Dr. Montague.

Mentally, having a buried penis can cause depression and self-esteem issues. “Most men associate their penis with their masculinity, and they associate fertility with masculinity,” says Dr. Dubin. “These kinds of issues can have strong mental health implications.”

IF YOUR BURIED penis is obesity-related, losing weight will definitely help. But keep in mind that, even after weight loss, you might have excess skin and tissue that keeps your penis buried.

“Sometimes, unfortunately, in very obese people, the damage has already been done, and more aggressive treatment options may be required,” Dr. Dubin. In that scenario, you may need surgery—which is also an option for people who don’t lose weight. Dr. Miller says surgeons would focus on removing the surrounding fat and tissue and repositioning the penis.

“That allows the penis to come outside of the body,” says Dr. Montague. “In most buried penis surgeries, skin and fat are cut out.”

Having a buried penis can be embarrassing to talk about, Dr. Dubin says. It's disturbing to have this happen and it can take a toll on your well-being. If you feel it's getting in the way of living fully, consider talking with a counselor or other mental health professional. It's also crucial to see a doctor if you’re having urinary, hygiene, or sexual health issues because of a buried penis.

Cassie Shortsleeve is a Boston-based journalist with more than a decade of experience reporting for some of the nation's largest print and digital publications, including Women's Health, Men's Health, Medscape, Parents, Conde Nast Traveler, and others. She also writes the best-selling Substack newsletter Two Truths and is a co-founder of the non-profit Chamber of Mothers.

#5036. Nvidia, Jensen Huang Sued by Shareholders Over Alleged Ties to Suspected Chinese Spy

Nvidia, Jensen Huang Sued by Shareholders Over Alleged Ties to Suspected Chinese Spy

Nvidia and CEO Jensen Huang have been sued by a shareholder who accuses them of defrauding investors by concealing Huang's alleged relationship with a suspected Chinese spy.

The proposed class action, filed in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of California on Monday, accuses Nvidia and Huang of making materially false and misleading statements about the company's dealings with Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer, according to the complaint. It alleges that Huang "had an affair with Alice Huang, a Megaspeed executive and suspected Chinese spy," and that the defendants failed to disclose that this raised national security concerns posing a material risk to Nvidia's reputation and business prospects.

The plaintiff, Mark Kistenmacher, seeks to represent investors who bought Nvidia stock between October 9, 2025, and November 8, 2026. The suit accuses Nvidia and Huang of violating Section 10(b) of the Securities Exchange Act and Rule 10b-5, and Huang of violating Section 20(a), which can hold executives liable alongside a company. It seeks unspecified damages and a jury trial. Kistenmacher is represented by The Rosen Law Firm.

The complaint targets four statements. Two came from Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo in October 2025, when he told The New York Times that Nvidia's employees had "found no evidence of diversion" at Megaspeed and that Huang's visits to customer events were routine. A third came from Rizzo last month, when he said Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were "not unusual or unexpected." The fourth was Huang's own description of Nvidia's dealings with Megaspeed as "normal and not unusual" in an interview with Bloomberg on October 30. Each was misleading, the suit alleges, because of Huang's relationship with Alice Huang.

Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, is not related to Jensen Huang, Reuters reported. The New York Times reported on Sunday that Nvidia opened its investigation after Huang acknowledged to the company's board that he had been intimate with Alice Huang. The newspaper also reported that U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio oversaw the drafting of a classified memo this spring warning top administration officials about the relationship, which was circulated ahead of U.S. President Donald Trump's trip to China in May.

Decrypt has reached out to Nvidia for comment. The allegations in the complaint have not been tested in court, and it is common for shareholders to sue companies after sharp share price declines.

The lawsuit was filed five minutes after U.S. markets closed on Monday, according to Reuters. Nvidia shares closed down 4.3% at $178.67 that day, according to Yahoo Finance, reducing its market value by about $190 billion, after having slid as much as 8% earlier in the session. The stock was flat on the day at $179.70 in premarket trading on Tuesday.

Nvidia's board opened an independent investigation on Sunday into reports that Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, saying it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets had been compromised. In an email to staff the same day, Huang apologized for being unfaithful to his wife, without naming the person involved.

The New York Times reported last month that U.S. officials had concluded Alice Huang was a Chinese intelligence asset, and the Financial Times later reported that a U.S. government memo said Jensen Huang had been romantically involved with her. China's embassy in Washington has dismissed the allegations as "groundless smears."

Trump weighed in on Monday. "I asked him about that and he said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy," the president told reporters, according to The Guardian. He added that the FBI was "looking into it in terms of something else," without elaborating. A day earlier, Trump told reporters he did not regret his decision not to act on the intelligence, and that he believed Huang should stay on as CEO. "I think he's an honorable guy," he said, according to the Times.

#5034. Alan Cumming Can’t Be Tamed

At 61, the Traitors host has made a career of playing by his own rules. From leading his dark new series Tip Toe to reprising his X-Men role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, he's always eager to take on a different form.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

“I’M GEARING UP towards hacking it all off,” Alan Cumming says as he twists a finger around an errant silver tendril of hair dangling across his forehead. “I usually like to end a job and do something—get it cut off or get it colored. Something.”

The 61-year-old Tony-winning-Broadway-star-turned-Emmy-winning-reality-TV-host is the consummate chameleon. In just the past year, he’s played the “dandy Scottish laird” host of The Traitors: New Blood, reprised his role as the X-Men’s resident lizard-like teleporting mutant Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, and took on one of his most emotionally and physically challenging roles as gay bar owner Leo in Tip Toe.

The impulse to change—both on screen and off—is therapeutic. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, I think, as a reaction to my childhood trauma about my father's obsession with my hair,” he says. Cumming’s memoir, Not My Father’s Son, opens with a scene of his father taking a rusty pair of sheep shears to his temple in a fit of rage. “They were blunt and dirty and they cut my skin, but my father shaved my head with them, holding me down like an animal,” Cumming writes in the first chapter.

The trauma followed him into early adulthood, and he experienced visceral anxiety whenever he got his hair cut. Eventually, however, the act of transformation transformed Cumming. “Hair was always something I felt I had no control over as a child,” he says. “So I wanted to have as much control over it and change it as much as possible, almost in a slightly perverse way.”

Of course, the choice to change isn’t always his, and sometimes the choice is taken away from him. It was “painful,” he says, to be unable to change his hair when he was working on certain projects, like his seven seasons on The Good Wife. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, but I don't often have my own hair,” Cumming adds. “It's usually for some job.”

Today, however, his natural silver hair seems purposefully unstyled as his fingers coax through it. That is, when he isn’t using his hands to assume the statuesque pose of his Traitors persona or demonstrate the boxing stance from his Marvel superhero training. Simply talking about a performance becomes a performance, his eyes bulging behind a pair of thick-rimmed black glasses and his arms gesturing widely underneath his faded red T-shirt as he describes the immense physicality required of these recent roles.

The walls of his Scottish home are white and surprisingly sparse. You’d have no idea that the person living there is now, by his own admission, best known for “swanning around” a Scottish castle “like an insane little prince” in increasingly outrageous outfits (so outrageous, in fact, that Traitors contestants often applaud when he enters the room after a wardrobe change). Or perhaps the walls are white so he stands out all the more. Either way, the Scottish charm is working overtime on a Sunday evening.

“I think both with my hair and, more lately with what I wear, I do sort of feel like a little dolly that people dress up,” he says before adding, with a good-natured laugh, “I don't mind it.”

In a brief moment between transformations, Cumming sat down with Men’s Health to discuss his ever-changing style on Traitors, how he trained for Avengers: Doomsday, and what he considers to be the ultimate fountain of youth.

MEN’S HEALTH: You often talk about your fashion choices with each Traitors season, but what goes into choosing your hairstyle?

ALAN CUMMING: There was a feeling that because we were doing the civilian version, we could reboot and bring things down a little. So, we decided just to go a little more simple and classic with the hair. But it was hilarious, because every morning I would walk downstairs and say, ‘This is toning it down. What the fuck?’ I guess I wasn't dressed as a coffin or a bride. The plan was to go back to the dandy Scottish laird a little, but it still was pretty mental. But that's why I didn't have lots of color and things in my hair. But we're about to start another Traitors season in a couple of weeks, so all bets are off again.

MH: You're in your 60s now, and you still have wonderful hair. Did you ever have a fear of going bald when you were younger, or would that have maybe been freeing in a way?

AC: I've never feared going bald. I don't think I would care. It's easy when you’re not the bald person saying this, but I don't quite think going bald has the same power it used to. I feel like so many people shave their heads nowadays anyway. And there are loads of actors I know who are bald and wear wigs all the time. When you're an actor, it's obviously easier—and actually probably better—to be bald, because you get more hairdo options.

MH: Outside of Traitors, you're also reprising your role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday. How was doing your own stunts in your 60s?

AC: I did some stunt work, but I didn't do all my own stunts. Please don't think that. There are lots of things I'm not allowed to tell you about. I got into trouble for giving the game away earlier on. But I mean, I fight some people. There's loads of fighting in it. If you enjoy fighting and you enjoy superheroes, this movie's for you.

I loved the training. They always train you for much more than you actually need, but I did it. I was boxing with these French boys. And I’d just made a film in France, so they were very impressed with my French and the fact that I could box a little and not have a heart attack.

It’s really funny when people, especially younger people, are so impressed by your fitness. I'm quite bendy as well, so people are always impressed by my flexibility. And I guess I am 61, but, I mean, I'm not dead. I'm not a thousand years old. I've luckily not had any sort of physical things going wrong. I don't really feel any different, to be honest, but it’s also hilarious when young people are just amazed that you've been able to stand up and not die.

MH: How long were you doing this boxing training for?

AC: I only did a week or so with those boys. But I used to take boxing lessons in New York, and I really want to get back into it again. It's such good exercise, especially before you actually get up and spar—all the core stuff and strength stuff—and it's such fun as well.

My boxing teacher was called Mo, and he'd been a big sort of world champion. But one day I went to the gym, and he was really pissed off because he’d seen a music video with some rappers and singers, and they were all playing boxers. He said, "They were just terrible."

And he went, "Drake. Drake was the worst."

And he said to me, "You could take Drake." So now, every time I see Drake, I just think, “I could take you.” Drake better watch out.

MH: Let him know it's on sight. How else did you prepare your body for Avengers?

AC: I actually stay pretty fit all the time. When I'm doing The Traitors, I go to the gym every day, or I do three days gym, two days swim or yoga. There have been times in my life when I've had to get really, really super fit or super skinny or something, so you go nuts. But I like being fit, and I like stretching. I like doing things like that, so I’m pretty constant.

Filming Tip Toe was much more physically demanding, because I knew I was going to have a lot of emotional weight to carry. It was a really disturbing show. So, I would exercise a lot, but I would also get two Thai massages on the weekend because my body was so tense.

If you do a film like X-Men and you're jumping around, yes, sometimes your body can get hurt in that way, but emotionally it wasn't really very demanding, whereas Tip Toe was so intense. Physically, I'm so stressed a lot of the time, and David Morrissey, the other actor, is shoving me around. So, my body was all screwed up. What's really important in those situations is to plan ahead and to make space for you to give your body some chance to recover.

MH: How do you think about your body differently as you take on each new role?

AC: I'm a very physical actor. I think about how the person physically behaves and how they move and stand and… [laughs]. I'm laughing because my husband is just over there, and he always watches me in things and says, "Oh, you look so different. You just disappeared into this character." And then there'll be a scene when I'm running, and he can tell it's me because I run in a weird way. My running always gives me away. But you know who's got the weirdest run ever? Woody Allen. I was once in a film with him and he had to run, and I've never seen anything like it. It just was like, "How is this all happening? How are all these limbs managing to propel someone forward?" It was the craziest thing.

But I'm always very conscious of the physicality of a character. The Traitors is a big example. He's very erect, very sort of grand, quite dancy. He moves like a dancer. And then in Tip Toe, it was really fascinating becoming more cowed as the show went on. He got less confident, so I got more crushed.

With Nightcrawler, he's really fascinating because he's like a little weird lizard or something. So it's a lot of neck stuff. In the first film, I did a lot of work with a movement person to develop the language of that. So, I just remembered all that stuff for Avengers. I suppose it’s muscle memory. After 22 years, I really did remember all that stuff. Also, they gave me a gray wig, which I thought was hilarious. That was a concession to my elder statesman-like thing. I've got gray hair as Nightcrawler now, which is just hilarious. Nothing else has changed, just his hair has grown gray.

MH: You mentioned your longtime husband, Grant. What do you think makes a relationship last?

AC: Having a laugh, being open and kind. Being kind is the biggest thing, but being honest and always trying to talk things through and having a laugh. Also, having time apart.

When I go away to make a film or whatever, we don't see each other for a few weeks. It's actually really great because you miss that person, and you look forward to seeing them and you value the time you do have. It's not just about missing them, but you remember the things that you've always liked, and you're constantly reassessing your relationship because you're looking forward to seeing that person again. Also, it means you maintain your own identity. Obviously, we're a very strong couple, but we have our own lives as well.

So it's a combination of those things, but I really do think you can't underestimate how much being able to laugh is. Also, I think it's good to still have sex. Sex and laughter. I think if either of those go or both of those go, get out, taxi for one. Because they're both the same thing, I suppose—they're sensual pleasures. Those are very important for me, anyway.

MH: Agreed. And so, you mentioned that you're 61 now—

MH: As we all should. But tell me, what's the best part about being 61?

AC: There are lots of nice things. I mean, partly that I forget I'm 61. On the show I'm doing right now, I feel like I can just chat with the kids on the crew who are in their 20s. For me, what's great is being in a position where I'm able to have such strong, long relationships, friendships. With Tip Toe, David Morrissey and I have known each other since the late '80s. Acting against each other in such a horrible way was so great because we were so close and trusted each other. And that was a really good thing about being older—you're so sure of someone and so trust someone. Also, I just love having a life where I do have friends in their 20s and 30s and 40s.

I equate oldness with lack of curiosity. You can be very old and still be very curious, but whenever anyone says, “Oh, I'm too old for this,” I think it's like a death knell somewhere. A little fairy dies when you say that.

I enjoy not giving so much of a fuck. I'm really happy in my life and things don't bother me as much as they used to. I think it'll all come out in the wash. We'll work it out. I feel I'm much more sanguine about things, especially as I've got such a crazy life with so many things going on. It's always this sort of mental schedule and trying to make it all work, but I just now think, “It'll be fine.” So that's a good thing about being older.

MH: What are you most curious about these days?

AC: Lots of things. I'm running a theater now and every day there are things I've got to deal with and talk about and find out about, and that's amazing. I joined TikTok today, so I decided to do that. I want to learn to play the bagpipes. I want to learn to type properly because, I mean, I use my computer, obviously, and I've written books and films, but I've never really properly learned to type. And I want to get better at Spanish, and I have a sort of trad wife fantasy of making gluten-free scones in my AGA, which is a kind of country stove.

AC: I don't really feel this age, but when I was 33, I moved to New York, and I feel like my life hasn't really changed that much since then. I still live in the same area. I still have a lot of the same friends. I do a lot of the same things. I have the same kind of life. I don't go out as much as I used to, but I love dancing still, and I would do that more if people didn't take photos of me all the time. Before mobile phone cameras, you didn't have to worry about being off your tits and dancing in a club with your chums. Now you do.

I'm obviously not 33. I don't look 33. But I feel like I'm as fit as I was when I was 33, kind of.

#5033. Binance Halts MEXC Transfers as FinCEN Cuts Exchange Off From US Financial System

Binance Halts MEXC Transfers as FinCEN Cuts Exchange Off From US Financial System

Binance has stopped processing transactions involving MEXC, one of the world's largest crypto exchanges, days after the U.S. Treasury Department designated MEXC a primary money laundering concern.

"Effective immediately, users should not send funds to, receive funds from, or otherwise transact through Binance involving MEXC, MEXC Global, MEXC affiliates or related entities," Binance tweeted on Tuesday.

Binance is applying enhanced compliance measures following FinCEN’s designation of MEXC as a Primary Money Laundering Concern.

Effective immediately, users should not send funds to, receive funds from, or otherwise transact through Binance involving MEXC, MEXC Global, MEXC…

Any request to send funds from a Binance account to MEXC will be declined, according to a Binance announcement that lists 13 entities covered by the restriction, including MEXC Global Limited, MEXC Estonia OÜ and MEXC Singapore Pte Ltd. Deposits received from those entities will be temporarily withheld for compliance review, and users could face further restrictions, including being offboarded from Binance, the exchange said.

MEXC ranks ninth among spot exchanges on CoinMarketCap, which scores platforms on traffic, liquidity and trading volume, with about $812.5 million in spot volume over the past 24 hours. Binance ranks first, with about $6.4 billion.

The Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) named MEXC a primary money laundering concern last Friday, in connection with Russian illicit finance. The order, issued under the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing fund transfers involving the exchange.

FinCEN alleges that MEXC processed at least $16 billion in suspicious transactions between May 2021 and May 2026, including nearly $1.5 billion in direct and indirect exposure to Russian sanctions evasion, and described its anti-money laundering controls as severely deficient.

The order takes effect 30 days after its publication in the Federal Register.

Binance is not the only exchange to respond. OKX has also warned its users against transferring funds to MEXC, Finance Magnates reported.

#5032. Everybody Wants to Do Shabbat Dinner at Erin Foster and Simon Tikhman’s Cozy, Playful LA Home

The recently dropped third season of the hit Netflix rom-com Nobody Wants This begins with a picture of domestic bliss: Kristen Bell and Adam Brody’s characters cheerfully lugging boxes and eating pizza sans dishes—living together at last. The fictional romance between the witty podcaster and “hot rabbi” is inspired by that of series creator Erin Foster and her husband Simon Tikhman, albeit loosely. Like Bell’s Joanne, Foster converted to Judaism before their wedding in 2019; unlike Brody’s Noah, Tikhman is a music executive, not a rabbi—but art is still intermingling with life.

As their onscreen alter egos feather their nest, Foster, Tikhman, and their two-year-old daughter Noa are starring in a new season of their own, moving into a 1920s Los Angeles home that mixes undefinable colors and bold pattern play with the “European flavor” of an old-world estate, per Tikhman. Researching the house’s roots, Foster found Spanish, Mediterranean revival, and English influences, and the couple felt free to embrace all three.

“I don’t tend to like following a set of rules,” Foster says, “whether it be in fashion or in television writing or in design.”

To create more of a grand entrance, the entry foyer was opened up on the second floor and “guests often mistake the entry tile as original,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The stair runner is by Stark and the vintage mirror is from Nickey Kehoe. The walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Soft Chamois.

Tikhman, Foster, and Noa seated on the custom living room sofas upholstered in a Schumacher fabric. “I had girls over for drinks and we sat in the room together,” Foster says. “It’s kind of like the gossip room.”

The sofas flank a custom coffee table in the living room. The vintage chair is from 1stDibs, the ottoman is upholstered in a fabric by Scalamandré, and the vintage floor lamp is from PRB Collection. The sconces are from Aqua Gallery and the curtain fabric is by Rose Tarlow Melrose House. The chandelier is by Cox London. “They handmake each metal leaf,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The artwork is by Danielle Orchard from Perrotin Gallery.

The multihyphenate cohost of The World’s First Podcast and cofounder of cool-girl fashion line Favorite Daughter acknowledges that she runs in a “circle of really fashion-forward women who have been featured in AD and have elevated taste in art and in design, and it’s very intimidating,” including when it came to selecting a designer. “There starts to be this culture of, ‘Well, you have to use this person,’” Foster says. Instead, she discovered her own on Instagram. Foster cold-messaged Joyce Pickens Dinwiddy, founder of LA’s JDP Interiors (and later the brains behind Favorite Daughter’s Beverly Hills boutique) about masterminding the new-old home’s four-year renovation and redesign.

“They’re a cool couple and they don’t want this stuffy, old-school, fully British-feeling home,” Pickens Dinwiddy says, but Foster and Tikhman still craved classicism their way—something “cozy, young, and playful but sophisticated at the same time.”

“I’ve realized I’m obsessed with the color green,” Foster says. “The color feels a little shocking sometimes to people, but it’s also inviting and it’s also kind of earthy and cozy.” The kitchen is painted in Denali Green from Dunn-Edwards and the cabinets are backed in a Rose Tarlow Melrose House fabric.

Tikhman requested and received a dedicated drinks fridge in the kitchen, filled with clean sodas and seltzers. “Simon was like, ‘I want a Kardashian fridge,’” Foster says.

The kitchen backsplash includes custom hand-painted tiles by Lascaux Tile and the hood is copper. The range is by Lacanche, the fridge is Sub-Zero, and the sink fittings are by Waterstone. The pendants are custom.

The result is a warm, hip house where Pickens Dinwiddy, who’s married to a Brit, introduced charming flourishes familiar to UK country homes. She softened cabinetry in the not-quite olive green kitchen with floral chintz fabric by Rose Tarlow Melrose House and added a skirt beneath the sink in the scullery, a hybrid mud room/potting room traditionally used to water herb pots from the garden. The glass-ceilinged breakfast nook—an “architectural feat,” according to Pickens Dinwiddy, that was lowered into the home via crane and which Foster says “probably added a year to the project”—has been crowned the solarium. With sun drenching its slate blue walls and sculpted medallion details, “it’s a really special place for us to have early dinner with our daughter,” Foster says, and also where she pops open her laptop, morning coffee in hand.

“Erin likes to wake up, have her coffee, pull up her computer, and sit in a room,” Tikhman says. “And her dream was that’s what the solarium was going to be.” The custom banquette is upholstered in a fabric from Perennials Fabrics and topped with pillows covered in Robert Kime fabric. The chairs by Casamidy are upholstered in a Perennials by Rose Tarlow fabric. The pendant is by Vaughan.

“I wanted to do wall-to-wall paneling somewhere,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. “This felt like the place for it so that there wasn’t a wall treatment competing with this beautiful tile floor.” The floor tile is by Ann Sacks. The mudroom bench is upholstered in a fabric by Décors Barbares.

The custom dining room table is surrounded by custom chairs covered in a Jasper fabric. The 1950s chandelier is from Fargo Design and the vintage Swedish sconces are from 1stDibs. The 1970s Danish sideboard is from Chairish and the mirror is custom.

I revel in the regal room names, but the couple is decidedly unfussy about their solarium and scullery—“words that Erin and I never used before building this house,” Tikhman says. “We actually only speak with English accents when we’re at home,” she cracks.

In fact, Foster applauds Tikhman for gently voicing concerns when, in the throes of the project, her old-world sensibilities started to feel a bit too old. Or as Tikhman jokes: “You wanted parking for our horse and carriage.”

“The man cave is my solarium,” Tikhman says. “It was my nonnegotiable, but with Erin’s blessing too.” The sofa is upholstered in Forenza Cinnamon velvet by Romo and the pillows are covered in a fabric by Zak + Fox. The coffee table is vintage.

The bar in the den features custom barstools covered in a Rogers & Goffigon fabric. The table lamp is vintage, the sink fittings are by Waterworks, and the walls are painted in Galway Bay by Dunn-Edwards.

“Simon was like, ‘Listen, I love you. I trust you. I know that you’re going to make this house beautiful, but I just keep thinking that I’m going to be walking around in basketball shorts and sneakers and a backwards hat and you’re in your pajamas all day long. Are we going to be in a castle that doesn’t feel like our personalities?’” Foster recalls. “It really woke me up to this idea that you have to balance everything.”

Out went an old-fashioned pull-chain toilet in the powder room; in came modern Swedish and Italian light fixtures, plus Tikhman’s passion project: making theirs a stealth smart home.

“The bedroom is very important because we’re people who like to be in the bedroom,” Tikhman says. He issues a clarification: “We like to watch TV in bed.” The bed is upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric and flanked with custom sconces. The shade fabric is by Jasper and the sofa is upholstered in a Claremont Furnishing fabric. The vintage pendant is from 1stDibs and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Gray Wisp.

In the primary bathroom, the hand-painted wall mural is by James Mobley. The vanity pendants are custom.

The vintage marble cabochon tiles are from Neolithic Materials. The tub is by The Water Monopoly.

Behind peony-printed Papier wallpaper and vintage sconces from 1stDibs, “the brains of the house are so advanced,” says Tikhman, cofounder and CEO of The Core Entertainment, a music company that includes artist management, publishing, a record label, and brand partnerships. He’s giddy over no-show speakers baked into the walls, a dining room mirror that slides open to reveal a TV viewable from the kitchen—so he can simultaneously watch and chat with Foster as she cooks her go-to tofu with broccoli (“Soy is no longer being vilified,” she says)—and an AI assistant named Nicola who can modulate temperature, lights, and of course music.

In the primary suite sitting room, the chair is upholstered in a Schumacher velvet and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Mustang.

Tikhman’s shoe collection in the primary closet

Alongside the revamp of their family home, the couple collaborated on season three of Nobody Wants This, with Foster writing, executive-producing, and making a cameo as an unhinged podcast fan (her impersonations in the writer’s room demanded to be seen), and Tikhman executive-producing the season-three soundtrack, curating unreleased tracks from the likes of Miley Cyrus and Ellie Goulding for the rom-com’s sweeping, big-love moments. Tikhman is used to managing artists, but jokes that producing the show’s soundtrack for the second time was tougher. “I want to make sure that my wife and her team are happy, and managing Erin is way more difficult,” he grins. “She’s my artist.”

“I’m trying to dress [Noa] into a cool tomboy, but she seems to really love putting on lots of jewelry and carrying a handbag,” Foster says. “I think we’ll adjust the room based on who she becomes.” In the nursery, the daybed is upholstered in a fabric by Namay Samay. The walls are covered in Lover’s Bloom by Papier and the pendant is by Beauvamp.

In Noa’s bathroom, the Hector Finch sconces feature hand-painted shades by artist Sarah Blomfield. The sink fittings are by Waterworks, the pendant is vintage, and the floor tiles are by Tabarka.

In a recent episode of Nobody Wants This, a pool party at Joanne and Noah’s spins out of control. The couple balks at first, but “I kinda like being the house that everybody comes to,” Joanne decides. Foster and Tikhman emphatically agree. “We do not want an untouchable home,” Foster says. “I don’t want to be a coaster house. I’m okay with a ring on the table.”

For a recent San Francisco 49ers game, Tikhman invited friends and Noa to “soft-launch” his “man cave,” a freestanding structure lined in Forenza Cinnamon velvet sofas by Roma meant to evoke the “Ralph Lauren vibes” of a high-end bar.

Foster says sharing a home with Tikhman “unlocked my inner trad-wife hostess.”

“I’m on a group chat called Shabbat Moms,” she says. Of its 12 members, “we’ve probably hosted more than anybody. We’ll have 30 people over, and there’s kids running everywhere, and it’s the best kind of chaos ever.”

Foster, Tikhman, and Noa in the garden designed by Emily Cooper with Balanced Ground Design.

#5031. HIVE, MARA mine more Bitcoin but earn less as crypto prices fall

Both miners reported higher Bitcoin production but lower mining revenue and rising electricity costs as they expanded their AI infrastructure businesses.

Update (Nov. 10, 2026, 7:31 am UTC): This article has been updated to include details of MARA’s $1.1 billion Bitcoin sale and its repayment of $750 million in Bitcoin-backed loans.

HIVE Digital Technologies and MARA Holdings mined more Bitcoin in the September quarter than a year earlier but reported lower mining revenue, as weaker Bitcoin prices outweighed gains in production.

According to their latest regulatory filings, HIVE’s Bitcoin production increased 49% to 1,070 Bitcoin (BTC), while mining revenue fell 7.4% to $76 million. MARA reported a similar pattern, with production rising 5% to 2,247 BTC and Bitcoin mining revenue dropping 34% to $159 million.

MARA also disclosed that it sold 13,597 BTC for about $1.1 billion after the quarter ended Sept. 30. It used $750 million of the proceeds to repay its Bitcoin-backed term loans in full, releasing 17,477 BTC it had pledged as collateral.

Both companies reported higher power bills and equipment charges. HIVE’s electricity costs rose 20% to $45.6 million, while MARA’s purchased energy costs increased 12% to $48.1 million as it expanded its owned mining operations.

HIVE also recorded a $31.2 million noncash write-down on mining equipment, citing “depressed mining economics,” while MARA separately wrote off $12 million involving mining rigs and equipment under restructuring plans.

Both results add to signs of pressure on Bitcoin mining economics. American Bitcoin last week reported weaker mining margins despite a treasury-driven quarterly profit, while Core Scientific said it plans to leave mining entirely by year-end.

Unlike Core Scientific, HIVE and MARA are both continuing to invest in Bitcoin mining alongside artificial intelligence and high-performance computing.

HIVE’s HPC hosting revenue rose 71% to $8.8 million in the September quarter, accounting for 10.4% of total revenue. The company has described its strategy as combining cash flow from mining with recurring revenue from AI infrastructure.

Related: American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

HIVE said it anticipates spending up to $493 million over 36 months to expand its HPC operations, including acquiring land, building out data center infrastructure and buying up GPUs. The company reported spending $145.3 million toward that expansion as of the filing.

HIVE also plans to spend about $35 million converting its Toronto facility into a Tier-III data center. Meanwhile, it continues to expand its mining infrastructure, targeting a 100-megawatt substation expansion at its Yguazú site in Paraguay during 2026.

MARA is also developing AI infrastructure through its Exaion subsidiary and a partnership with Starwood Digital Ventures. It said it intends to operate sites that can shift between workloads depending on economics, demand and grid conditions, with Bitcoin mining remaining the foundation of its platform.

HIVE’s Nasdaq-listed shares fell 1.7% to $1.78 in after-hours trading Monday, according to Google Finance, after closing the regular session up 1.7% at $1.81.

The company reported fiscal second-quarter revenue of $84.9 million, down 2.7% from a year earlier but above the $79.4 million consensus estimate listed by MarketBeat.

Meanwhile, MARA reported net income attributable to common stockholders of $634.4 million, up from $123.1 million a year earlier, and included substantial fair-value gains on digital assets and digital asset receivables.

MARA reported quarterly revenue of $164.2 million, down 35% year-on-year, and diluted earnings of $1.28 a share, up from 27 cents, while its shares were little changed at $6.64 in after-hours trading Monday after closing up 4.6%.

Both connect through Bluetooth and can switch between up to three paired devices, handy when you’re bouncing between your work laptop and personal computer.

See more of that spreadsheet without constantly scrolling. This 28.2-inch monitor has a taller screen than most widescreen displays, giving you extra room for documents or two windows side by side. Text looks crisp, and a matte finish helps cut reflections. Brightness adjusts to the room, while a light behind the screen adds a soft glow to your desk. One USB-C connection can carry your laptop’s picture and charge it at the same time.

Finding your wallet just got easier. This 1.7-millimeter-thick tracker slips into a card slot without the lump of a round tracker and works with Apple Find My or Google Find Hub. If it’s hiding nearby, ring the built-in alarm to narrow the search. Wireless charging saves you from replacing batteries, with up to five months between charges. A small addition that could save you a frantic morning.

#5028. Binance Warns of Apple iOS Flaw That Could Expose iPhone Users' Private Keys

Binance Warns of Apple iOS Flaw That Could Expose iPhone Users' Private Keys

Binance has warned iPhone users about a vulnerability in Apple's iOS that it says could let malicious apps read files stored by other apps, putting crypto wallet seed phrases, private keys and login credentials at risk.

The flaw, dubbed "worse_query," affects iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1 and 27.2, the exchange tweeted on Tuesday. "This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance," it said.

A vulnerability (worse_query) affecting iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1, and 27.2 allows malicious apps to read local files from other apps, putting seed phrases, private keys, and login credentials at risk across the entire ecosystem. This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance.…

Binance urged users to delete any apps installed from untrusted sources, such as sideloaded or manually signed app files, and to update iOS and all apps to the latest official versions. Anyone who suspects a breach should move their funds to a new wallet created on a clean device and reset all active login sessions, it said.

iPhone apps normally run inside a sandbox, which stops them from accessing other apps' private files. A researcher who goes by forcequitOS published worse_query on GitHub as a sandbox escape for iOS 26.0 through 26.7.2 and iOS 27.2, describing it as a proof of concept for developers that "isn't useful by itself." Among the locations it can reach is the folder where iOS apps store their data, according to the repository, which calls it the "direct successor" to an earlier exploit, bad_query. There have been no confirmed reports of crypto being stolen using the flaw.

Apple released iOS 27.2.1 on Monday, a day after the proof of concept was published. Its security notes list fixes for nine vulnerabilities, including an authorization issue in App Intents through which "an app may be able to access sensitive user data," as well as several Bluetooth and iMessage flaws.

However, the notes do not mention worse_query or its researcher, and Apple has not publicly confirmed whether the update addresses the flaw Binance described. At least one security expert believes it does. 23pds, chief information security officer at blockchain security firm SlowMist, urged users to update to iOS 27.2.1, saying in a tweet that the update would block the worse_query sandbox escape, along with other threats.

It is not the first time attackers have targeted Apple devices. Earlier this year, Google warned of iOS malware that targeted crypto apps on unpatched devices, while in August, the Dutch national cyber agency said hackers were exploiting a flaw in Apple's macOS Screen Sharing feature to plant Monero miners on Macs.

#5026. 15 Chic and Surprising Christmas Tree Decorating Ideas

Nothing says the holidays have arrived like a glowing pine strung with tinsel. If you’re looking for fresh inspiration, this collection of designer-approved Christmas tree decorating ideas has it all. From playful color palettes to chic lighting ideas and nostalgic personalization, designers are sharing their best tips for decorating full-sized, pencil, and even tabletop trees. Like a freshly pruned fir, their ideas are lush and abundant: oversized bows, classic red ornaments, blue Christmas trees, and velvet ribbon turned into handmade ornaments are just a few of their favorite festive touches. Whether you prefer a real tree, artificial Christmas tree, or even a flocked Christmas tree, lean minimalist or fully embrace maximalist holiday decor, we’ve rounded up our favorite Christmas decorating ideas to help you deck your halls in style.

To personalize your tree, take inspiration from Chanel Sansregret of the Charleston-based Studio Miles Design who purchases frame ornaments with the year to fill with photos of her children every holiday season. “For me, there’s something especially moving about hanging those memories on the branches and seeing so many stages of their childhood together in one place,” she shares. “As a designer, I love a beautifully considered tree, but the sentimental details are what give it soul,” Sansregret states.

“There’s something so special about turning a corner and finding another beautifully decorated tree,” says Emily Hewett of A Well Dressed Home based in Dallas and Kansas City. In her son’s bedroom, she opted for a slender pencil tree given the room’s space constraints. “My son’s tree sits directly in front of his bedroom window, which faces the street; one of my favorite details is seeing it all lit up from the front of our home at night,” Hewett says. A pencil tree has a small footprint, yet Hewett notes it brings a little extra Christmas magic to both the inside and outside of the home.

To make a Christmas tree feel like an integral part of a room, take design cues from the room itself. Here, Hewett adorned her tree with a collection of glass bauble Christmas ornaments that she’s curated over the years. “The color palette features varying shades of blue, green, and pink, along with clear iridescent ornaments that almost resemble delicate bath bubbles,” she says. The Christmas theme was inspired by her living room palette. “Every color was intentionally pulled from the wallpapers and fabrics in my living room, creating a Christmas tree that feels like a natural extension of the space,” Hewett says.

When decorating your Christmas tree in a room with dark walls, Renee DiSanto of the Chicago area–based Park & Oak recommends using lots of twinkle lights and weaving them into the tree in a zigzag pattern. “I tend to opt for a warm white light rather than a cool blue tone for a more cozy, inviting look,” she shares. Here, the designer used bright colored decorations, a bead garland, a simple tree skirt—and plenty of twinkle lights. “A well-lit tree will always pop against dark walls,” DiSanto says.

“Bows are such a classic, but including them on a tree in a dramatic scale is what makes something traditional feel unexpected and even a bit whimsical,” says Amy Studebaker of Amy Studebaker Design. She approaches Christmas tree decorating the same way she approaches designing a home: ”With a fresh take on traditional, layers and layers of charm, and a little sprinkle of the unexpected.” For a fun and festive look, replace a traditional tree topper with a large bow and adorn branches with smaller, coordinating bows.

Brown has become the neutral this season, adding cozy sophistication to interiors and now, to holiday decor. Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective loves the color for its warmth and versatility, and used it as the primary color on this tree. “I layered matte and metallic brown ornaments with neutrals—think muddy hues of bone, chocolate, and champagne—to create depth and avoid a flat look,” Casey shares. While the metallics catch light, the browns ground the palette, resulting in a tree that Casey says is both elevated and inviting.

To add charm and a touch of whimsy to a Christmas tree, Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective looks for ornaments that feel collected and tell a story, whether they’re vintage finds, handmade pieces, or unique shapes. “In order to make it feel cohesive, I recommend mixing textures and scales, like pairing glass baubles with fabric or wood. It brings a sense of personality and keeps the tree from feeling too uniform,” she shares. Casey also loves incorporating dynamic elements like ribbon or bells to add movement and playfulness.

What is the correct order to decorate a Christmas tree?

The first thing in Griffin’s step-by-step approach is adding twinkle lights to the tree, then tucking in larger ornaments. “Next, I add the smaller decorative ornaments, and finally, I add the tinsel or bows on the edges of the tree limbs,” Griffin shares. Studebaker says you should have fun with decorating and not take the process too seriously; “It can still be sophisticated, but it should absolutely be a happy and playful process along the way,” the designer says.

What are the most popular Christmas tree decorations?

Designers say German glass ornaments, Christopher Radko ornaments, cascading ribbon, festive bows, and warm white lights are among the most popular Christmas tree decorations. Metallic decorations, whether gold, silver, or copper, are another favorite. “I love decorating with metallic ornaments because they reflect the light so beautifully,” Bonney-James shares.

For Bonney-James, a timeless Christmas theme includes a fresh tree. “I’ll trade messy needles for an aromatic, nostalgic experience any day,” she says. Another must-have are warm white lights, which is why she recommends checking the temperature of LED lights to make sure they don’t appear blue. The addition of silver, gold, and white decorations completes the timeless look and adds festive shine.

#5024. OpenAI builds kill switch to stop rogue agents within 35 seconds

Control Room cuts off model output for internal research jobs when people or automated monitors flag safety or security concerns.

OpenAI has built a system that can cut off an AI research experiment’s access to model output, following several disclosures of its models acting beyond their instructions this year.

In a blog post Monday, OpenAI called the system “Control Room,” which is a centralized system that tracks model workloads across its research infrastructure and stops them quickly when safety or security concerns arise.

“As our models get more powerful and the associated safety and security risks become bigger, it becomes more and more critical that we can detect potential issues, and quickly stop models in their tracks when needed,” said the company.

The announcement comes after OpenAI disclosed several incidents this year in which research models acted outside their instructions during evaluations. In July, the company said its models had escaped a restricted testing environment and hacked Hugging Face to cheat on a security evaluation.

In October, it disclosed that a research model had used a crypto wallet without authorization to pay for smart contract deployments using testnet Ether, then omitted the spending from its final summary.

Control Room currently covers OpenAI’s internal research workloads, such as training experiments, evaluations and data processing.

It works by assigning each research job an identifier, which the inference engine checks before generating model output. If a person or automated monitoring system orders the job stopped, Control Room revokes its authorization, blocking further output even if the underlying research program keeps running, according to OpenAI.

OpenAI said cached authorizations could delay a shutdown by up to 35 seconds. The system is also designed to reject new requests if Control Room becomes unavailable.

#5023. Morning Minute: Vitalik Recasts ETH as a Store of Value

Morning Minute: Vitalik Recasts ETH as a Store of Value

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🏛️ Vitalik Recasts ETH as a Store of Value

Vitalik Buterin spent Sunday on X telling Ethereum holders to stop valuing ETH as a fee-burning token and start valuing it as a store of value. “We should not feel sad if fee burn is less than 10% of breakeven indefinitely,” he wrote. “The value of ETH comes from elsewhere.”

That elsewhere is stability, which he ties to decentralization, censorship resistance and security. It replaces the ultrasound money pitch, the idea that network usage burns supply faster than staking creates it. The burn has not kept up in years. Through the first nine months of 2026 it offset about 2% of issuance while supply grew 0.64%, mostly because Ethereum’s own rollup roadmap moved activity and fees off the mainnet.

Buterin’s argument is that this is fine, and that chasing the burn would be worse. Maximizing fee revenue means wanting congestion and expensive blockspace. He warned it would turn Ethereum into “a world-scale major corporation” at the cost of what makes it worth using. The burn, he added, was “never a true floor” and came mostly from internal crypto churn like lending and “silly NFT purchases.”

Supporters are already building out the new frame. Ethlabs co-founder Binji Pande calls ETH a future-proof store of value and argues it beats Bitcoin on quantum and AI risk, citing eleven years of trading history and an Ethereum Foundation that holds under 0.2% of supply. Julian Ma of Ethlabs makes the related case that Ethereum’s security culture positions it as a programmable store of value as AI pressures cryptography.
